Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often exhibit sycophancy, altering their answers to align with user suggestions even on objective factual and reasoning tasks. Yet the quantitative form of this influence--- and whether it is calibrated to source reliability--- remains poorly understood. In this work, we investigate this behavior through hint-induced answer shifts on MMLU questions, evaluating prompts of the form ``[Source] thinks the answer is (X)''. We systematically vary the source to which the hint is attributed (a professor, a friend, or a dog) across three open-source model families, Qwen, Gemma, and SmolLM. We show that the change induced by the hint follows a predictable structure well-characterized by an exponential tilt: the hint induces an approximately constant mean shift in the hinted answer's log-odds, with question--target susceptibility largely shared across sources. To explore how this pattern can arise, we introduce a formal decomposition of the hint response and illustrate in controlled synthetic experiments how KL-regularized reward-based training reshapes both hinted and unhinted answer distributions. Finally, we evaluate whether this shift is calibrated to source reliability, finding that the log-odds shift persists even when the hint-giver is declared inaccurate or accompanied by explicit warnings not to trust the source, highlighting the vulnerability of LLMs to adversarial steering.

</details>

Abstract: Participating in a conversation involves not only deciding what to say but also whether to speak at all. This judgment carries even greater significance in environments like the Werewolf game, where participants' interests conflict, because both utterances and silence are subject to interpretation and assessment by others. Therefore, determining when to speak, as well as what to say, is crucial for an LLM agent playing the Werewolf game. In this study, we introduce a Werewolf AI agent developed for the Anytime Utterance Track of the AIWolfDial shared task. Our system builds on the existing Inner Thoughts framework, which determines utterance timing during conversations by generating internal thoughts and evaluating the value of each thought for expression at that moment. Since existing methods were designed for general dialogue, we have redesigned the system specifically for the competitive environment and incomplete information setting of Werewolf games. We call our adapted method Adversarial Inner Thoughts. Through experiments involving analysis of self-play logs and human evaluations, we analyze and discuss the effectiveness of our approach.

</details>

Abstract: Language models can answer a question from a supplied document or from their parametric knowledge. When later asked where the answer came from, what determines their source report? We study this by intervening on the model's key-value (KV) cache after the answer is generated and before the source question is asked. On a controlled factual task with four models (7B-32B), changing or removing the document's cache never changes the report. In contrast, transplanting the question-and-answer (Q+A) cache between runs with identical question and answer tokens transfers it: every eligible report follows the donor, while the recipient's document is unchanged. On the two smaller models, steering Q+A values along directions fitted before any source question reverses 98.9-100% of reports across four document conditions at a fixed strength, while norm-matched random directions flip at most 1.1%. The components of keys and values along fitted directions carry the transplant effect: copying them alone nearly reproduces the report change; holding them fixed nearly prevents it. Applied during answer generation, the same value directions also change which source supplies the answer, though more weakly and depending on the prompt. Source reporting in this setting thus depends causally on cached state produced while answering. We do not establish general reporting accuracy or a complete interpretation of this state.

</details>

Abstract: One approach to accelerating progress on AI alignment is to automate parts of alignment research itself. Doing so requires systems that can perform reliably on hard-to-supervise research tasks, including identifying flaws in research proposals and evaluating whether a proposed experiment supports its intended claim. To evaluate this capability, we introduce Tampered ML, a benchmark of 1,858 item-pairs, each consisting of an experiment proposal reconstructed from a published machine learning paper and a minimally edited counterfactual that introduces a targeted methodological flaw. Models must identify the more methodologically sound proposal in both presentation orders. A human validation study on a stratified sample estimates that 86% of items are valid (95% CI: 81-91%). Frontier models perform above chance but remain far from reliable (with Opus 5 achieving 77.9% accuracy). They are highly sensitive to the order of presentation and favor flawed designs that appear more specific, transparent, or conventional.

</details>

Abstract: Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a powerful paradigm for scaling Neural Machine Translation (NMT) and managing language heterogeneity. However, practical limitations hinder its deployment on resource-constrained devices: the entire expert pool must remain in memory, and the per-token compute budget is statically fixed during training by rigid top-k routing. We introduce EDWARD-NMT (Elastic Dimensions With Adaptive Routing and Dual-consistency for NMT), a novel MoE encoder-decoder architecture designed for a train once, deploy anywhere paradigm that exposes a family of deployable sub-networks at inference time. EDWARD-NMT integrates three key innovations: (i) Matryoshka-style elastic dimensions to flexibly reduce the memory footprint; (ii) an adaptive sigmoid router with a learned, task-conditioned threshold, allowing each token to dynamically determine its active expert count; and (iii) a dual-consistency objective to maximize the performance of the extracted sub-networks. Extensive experiments on PhoMT (En-Vi), WMT-14 (En-De), multilingual OPUS-16, and a multi-domain De-En benchmark validate our approach. The full-capacity architecture matches or exceeds strong MoE baselines with improved parameter efficiency. Furthermore, the sub-networks seamlessly extracted from a single checkpoint consistently outperform comparably sized dense Transformers without any retraining.

</details>


### [217] [Semantically Rich, Metrically Affect-Blind: A Geometric Blind Spot in Modern Text Embedders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32122)

Abstract: We report a systematic dissociation in modern text embedders: sentiment is almost perfectly decodable from their embeddings, yet almost entirely absent from their metric. Across eleven frozen encoders spanning 2021 to 2026, including 2026-generation models and the 4B-parameter LLM embedder Qwen3-Embedding-4B, a linear probe recovers sentence polarity at up to 91.3% accuracy while cosine similarity separates polarity by a margin of only 0.003-0.064. Qwen3-Embedding-4B, the strongest probe in the panel, reaches 91.3% probe accuracy but only 64.3 retrieval MAP. Because retrieval, clustering, deduplication, and retrieval-augmented generation operate on the metric rather than on a trained probe, these systems are effectively blind to information their encoder demonstrably contains. A task-conditioning control suggests this reflects the pretraining objective rather than a prompting artifact: conditioning Jina-v5 on its classification task rather than its retrieval task recovers only about half of the MAP gain that injection achieves on the same weights (63.2 to 73.8, versus 85.1), and leaves the alignment gap essentially unchanged. Sentiment-Injected Sentence Embeddings (SISE) train a small (0.13M-0.69M-parameter) supervised-contrastive head on a frozen encoder in seconds on a CPU, using the labelled training set. The head improves every encoder released since 2022 on full-data probe accuracy (up to +5.8 points), retrieval MAP (up to +26.0) and polarity margin (7-61x). This is a specialization, not a free gain: the compact 128-d head loses 38-41 Spearman points on STS-B, while a fused variant that concatenates it with the base embedding loses 2.4-12.0. With the head, a 0.6B encoder at 128 dimensions matches a 4B encoder given the same head (91.8 vs. 90.3 MAP). We release a CPU-reproducible pipeline and a reusable diagnostic, the Attribute Alignment Gap.

</details>

Abstract: RAG and retrieval-augmented few-shot ICL can struggle when decision-relevant evidence is buried in long, noisy context. Retrieved passages and demonstrations may contain redundant or distracting information. Although expert-labeled development examples may identify the text supporting each decision, these annotations are typically used only as answer-side rationales rather than as supervision for filtering noisy retrieval. We propose silver-evidence demonstrations, which replace raw retrieved passages in ICL examples with generated, target-conditioned evidence summaries. For each labeled development example, an LLM generates silver evidence without access to the expert label or gold evidence; this evidence is then paired with the expert label and gold evidence where available. At test time, a single structured call generates silver evidence from retrieved passages and then predicts the final label and decision evidence. Across EI 2.0, SciFact, synthetic policy retrieval, and HICRIC/IMR data, our full method improves over equally sized gold-only ICL (label-only for policy) on three of four datasets and improves evidence F1 on all annotated datasets. It also achieves higher mean label accuracy than InstructRAG-style ICL on all four datasets. These results suggest that replacing demonstration-side retrieved passages with compact, target-conditioned silver evidence is particularly useful for decisions based on long and noisy text, where it reduces distracting context while preserving decision-relevant signals and expert supervision.

</details>


### [219] [Shorter Is Not Cheaper: A Controlled Comparison of Declarative Animation Specifications and Free-Form Code for LLM-Generated Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32129)

Abstract: Chat assistants increasingly answer conceptual questions with interactive, animated explanations, generated either as free-form HTML/JavaScript or as declarative specifications that a fixed runtime plays. The declarative route is widely expected to be cheaper, faster and more reliable, because its outputs are shorter and constrained. We test this expectation in controlled comparisons whose hypotheses and analysis plans were frozen before the runs. We designed ExplainSpec, a time-indexed declarative animation format, and an observation contract that makes every generated explanation programmatically inspectable; validated, pacing-agnostic checkers score 20 explanation requests from five domains. Three Claude models (360 jobs) and, in a follow-up, the open-weight Qwen3-14B with thinking enabled and disabled (240 jobs) answered every request in both formats. For every model the specifications were 25-48% shorter in answer tokens (output excluding reasoning), yet with reasoning enabled total output fell significantly only for the largest Claude model and no model generated significantly faster. The reasons differed. The Claude models reasoned far more before writing specifications (exploratory; for the two larger models, from 15-20% to 85-88% of generations). Qwen3-14B reasoned about as much in both formats (a pre-registered test of this shift was not supported), and its shorter answers saved little against a reasoning trace more than twice as long. Nor were specifications more reliable: free-form pages were valid after one repair round in 95-100% of jobs for every model, whereas Qwen3-14B produced valid specifications in only 33% of jobs and passed all semantic tests in 17% (HTML/JavaScript: 62%). For reasoning models, a shorter format alone therefore does not make generation cheaper, faster or more reliable, and a format learned in context can cost reliability.

</details>

Abstract: Election Commitment Mining refers to the analysis of election commitments made by electoral candidates or political parties during election campaigns. However, many elected candidates and political parties, especially in developing South Asian countries, do not fully deliver on the promises they make before elections. Even after the elections, commitments presented through election manifestos, posters, pamphlets, videos, and other campaign materials are often not systematically or transparently archived. As a result, these promises tend to fade away over time, making it difficult to hold the elected candidates accountable for their commitments. To address this persistent issue, this research aims to develop a system capable of structuring the commitments made by candidates during election periods. In this work, we created the first gold-standard dataset of Nepali political texts (Cohen's kappa = 0.777) and evaluated machine learning, deep learning and large language models to automatically extract and analyze election commitments. LaBSE-LR achieved the best results among machine learning models (0.897 accuracy, 0.896 macro F1-score, 0.947 AUROC), while fastText-LSTM achieved the highest performance among deep learning models (0.914 accuracy, 0.913 macro F1-score, 0.961 AUROC). Among the three evaluated large language models, Qwen3-8B performed best in the few-shot setting (0.848 macro F1-score). The outcome of this work is integrated into a mobile application, where commitments can be visualized with the support of GAI, enhancing transparency, civic engagement, and electoral accountability.

</details>

Abstract: When a language model is asked whether its own answer is correct, three languages are involved: that of the question, that requested for the answer, and that of the verifier instruction asking for the judgment. Multilingual confidence evaluations seldom separate the three. We vary them independently in a six-language factorial design with ten open-weight models (3B-14B) on LSQA, MMMLU and TriviaQA, holding each answer fixed across verifier languages and measuring confidence with a first-token P(True) probe. The three languages move different parts of the evaluation: which of them governs accuracy depends on the benchmark, matching the response language to the question or verifier language sharpens discrimination but not calibration, and the verifier language alone shifts calibration error by up to 0.04 without changing accuracy. The scorer matters as well: an unvalidated judge would have shrunk the MMMLU English-Hindi accuracy gap from 0.198 to 0.038. Multilingual confidence results, model rankings included, are therefore comparable only when all three languages are reported and the scorer is validated.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model agents that use tools could take on part of the electronic health record and desk work that occupies physicians' time, but in many hospitals the medical information systems run inside closed networks, so the model and the agent harness must also sit inside. We designed, implemented, and deployed LLENS, a clinical support AI agent platform that runs inside the closed network of Hokkaido University Hospital on one host with 8 NVIDIA H200 GPUs, the open-weight model Kimi K2.6 (~1 trillion parameters), and a web application rebuilt from Open WebUI. The harness has 10 components, including page image reading, full-text attachment input, a path-addressed document tree without a vector index, a network-isolated sandbox, artifact return, self-checking of generated output, subagents, and saved procedures. Kimi K2.6 was chosen from 3 open-weight candidates and scored 480/500 on the 2026 Japanese Medical Licensing Examination; it generated 119 tokens/s for one user and served 16 concurrent requests at 908 tokens/s in total. On 180 questions over hospital documents (3 seeds each), mean accuracy was 10.7% with no documents, 81.3% with fixed retrieval (top 10 chunks of one lexical search), and 97.2% with interactive access, in which the model reads with document tools and the sandbox (15.9 percentage points above fixed retrieval, 95% CI 10.9-21.3); the difference lay in filtering/counting questions (16.7% vs 87.8%). Five scenarios with synthetic data ran on combinations of the components: needlestick injury response, batch adverse event grading, scanned referral letter transcription, falls data aggregation and plotting, and investigation of 3 topics by subagents. The platform has run on the hospital network since September 14, 2026.

</details>

Abstract: A collection date records when a source revision was acquired, not when its contents changed. Can assigning this metadata redirect temporal question answering despite unchanged evidence? Swapping the question day and following day between two revisions lowers later-reference exact match (EM) by 37.6-51.4 percentage points across five open-weight LLMs on 595 Wikipedia pages, replicated on 128 disjoint pages. Previous-day swaps retain 29.0-39.6-point losses on 497 pages. Seven prompt variants, including explicit ignore-collection instructions and archived-revision provenance, retain the effect. Fictional answers and English month-name question dates show that remembered answers and identical date strings are unnecessary. Matched thinking reduces Qwen3.5's penalty from 30.1 to 11.1 points, while native reasoners vary. Two API LLMs show small Wikipedia swap effects and rarely shift to earlier answers on fictional records. Large swap-induced losses concentrate around the question date; swaps between distant dates have much smaller effects. Adding fields containing one observed acquisition date lowers EM relative to omission in three of five LLMs after correction. The diagnostic isolates metadata effects from changing evidence or missing the relevant revision, and provides a practical audit for temporal QA inputs.

</details>


### [224] [SOPO: Socratic Guided Policy Optimization for Span-Level Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32212)

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have gained widespread adoption in various natural language processing tasks, but suffer from hallucination issues where they generate unfaithful or inconsistent content. While Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has shown promising applications in reasoning tasks, its application to span-level hallucination detection is challenged by localization ambiguity, reward sparsity on hard examples, and uncontrolled length inflation. To address these issues, we introduce Socratic Guided Policy Optimization (SOPO), a multi-turn RL framework for robust hallucination detection. The key innovation of SOPO is a Socratic feedback mechanism, where a teacher model guides the reasoning trajectories during rollout by providing indirect hints inferred from the ground truth. This teacher-guided approach enables the model to generate more effective reasoning trajectories for hard examples, thus alleviating reward sparsity. Furthermore, we incorporate Turn-based Advantage Scaling to penalize verbosity bias, encouraging the model to develop efficient and autonomous reasoning capabilities. Empirical results on RAGTruth demonstrate that SOPO (4B) achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing leading proprietary models (e.g., GPT-5) and standard RL baselines, while obtaining higher reported mean scores than GRPO on four out-of-domain benchmarks, with mixed evidence against CAPO. We release our training and evaluation code alongside the 1.7B and 4B SOPO models at https://github.com/patsnap/SOPO.

</details>

Abstract: We propose DIAL-S2ST, a post-training method for learning inference-time latency control in simultaneous speech-to-speech translation models. Our approach reuses pretrained translation capabilities through self-distillation from lookahead predictions, while limiting the influence of large text distillation losses to account for the imitation gap. This provides supervision for earlier emission without paired target data or reward computation. To make the learned timing adjustable at inference, we jointly train a single LoRA adapter across scales with matched lookahead guidance. Experiments on French-to-English translation with Hibiki demonstrate that only 10 hours of source speech enable this adaptation, providing flexible latency control with a favorable quality-latency trade-off.

</details>

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Looped language models run each token through the same layers several times, and each run is one iteration. To save compute, an extra layer, the halting head, gives after each iteration the probability that the token stops there, so that easy tokens can stop early. Pretraining models from scratch, up to 1.3B parameters, we find two problems. First, heads trained on a mix of all iterations predict that tokens need under half the iterations, yet under the standard stopping rule tokens run almost every iteration, because this training never scores the single iteration where a token stops. Scoring every iteration instead fixes this, but in our runs it works only with a strong penalty favoring early stops. Second, savings are commonly reported as an average number of iterations per token, but when sequences are generated in batches that run until their slowest token stops, a model without a head is almost as good at that cost. The reported average can therefore overstate the saving. We recommend reporting the iterations tokens actually run, with the stopping threshold and batch size, and give a test, run before training a head, that bounds its gain.

</details>


### [227] ["Not" is Not Lost in Translation: A Causal Analysis of Negation Representations in Multilingual LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32281)
*Giang Son Nguyen,Wray Buntine,Dung D. Le,Wenya Wang*

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: Negation occurs in every language, yet its surface realization differs drastically. Despite this, multilingual large language models (LLMs) translate negation robustly, raising the question of whether they share an underlying representation of negation across languages. We study this question in six open models across English, French, Vietnamese, and Chinese, using causal interventions on a synthetic contrastive dataset and a negation translation dataset derived from FLORES-200. Across models, we find strong evidence that negation representations are causally controllable and largely shared across languages. In particular, we show that a contrastive activation direction extracted from one language can induce or remove negation in the others, and directions extracted from different languages are substantially aligned. We further find that negation is represented in sparse features: a single sparse autoencoder (SAE) feature per model suffices to control polarity across languages, while responding selectively to negated inputs. Finally, both dense and sparse interventions generalize to four additional languages and FLORES-derived sentences. We release data and code to support future work: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/negation-translation/.

</details>


### [228] [Many Perturbations, Shared Failure Dynamics: Tracing Direction-Sensitive Perturbation Influence in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32309)

Abstract: Why can a task-preserving rewrite turn a correct answer into a failure while a larger internal change leaves the answer intact? We develop a direction-sensitive, readout-relative account of semantic fragility: heterogeneous language changes can share geometric and readout relationships without sharing an input-independent failure direction. Clean decision reserve, representation transformation, and response alignment provide complementary descriptions of this process. An exact longitudinal-transverse decomposition identifies directional information discarded by scalar transmission. A compact reserve-and-transmission model predicts failures across held-out families and items in Qwen3-32B and Gemma 4 31B, with useful but parameterization-dependent transfer to ReClor. Matched interventions show that orientation carries causal information beyond scalar geometry. Among naturally failed rewrites, attention-component tests on disjoint original items and subsequent answer-order controls favor the natural paired direction over tested random and same-answer-letter donor directions. Yet failures rarely persist across option orders, and current-order corrections use recomputed clean references. Shared predictive regularity thus coexists with context-dependent directional effects. Numerical path integration reduces, but does not eliminate, finite-patch approximation error within a fixed context; a larger discrepancy from the natural rewrite effect remains. A complementary fixed-CoT cue study extends this account to influence unacknowledged in recorded reasoning: readout associations transfer across domains, although the positive-mean causal criterion fails and signed-effect prediction remains post hoc. Together, these results support shared relations among reserve, transformation, and response while distinguishing local causal sensitivity from a complete explanation of natural failure.

</details>


### [229] [System One models Are Not Better Rerankers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32318)

Abstract: System One models, or decision models, answer a question about an input with a calibrated probability instead of generated text. Within weeks of the first release, in September 2026, they were used as rerankers. We test whether the decision fine-tuning behind them makes a better reranker than plain reranker fine-tuning of the same model. For six Qwen backbones from 0.6B to 27B parameters and ten TREC DL and BEIR collections, we compare the untuned model, asked zero-shot, with its decision fine-tunes and its reranker fine-tunes, two of which we train ourselves. In our work, we draw the conclusion that decision fine-tuning never makes the better reranker. From 0.6B to 9B, reranker fine-tuning wins on every backbone by 5 to 29 points nDCG@10, also on zero-shot collections, and at 27B the two tie. Up to 9B, decision fine-tuning adds nothing over simply asking the untuned model. Moreover, we find that the calibration advantage of decision models depends on the question. Asked whether a passage answers the query, they are better calibrated than the published reranker fine-tunes from 4B on. Asked a slightly different question from prior work, they lose this advantage. Lastly, calibration does not make up for a worse ranking: up to 9B, a reranker fine-tune keeps the better passages at a fixed threshold. If you need a reranker, fine-tune a reranker.

</details>


### [230] [Beyond Final Outcomes: Quantifying Claim-Level Information Dynamics in Multi-Agent LLM Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32333)
*Haocheng Jiang,Herun Wan,Yongyuan Peng,Minnan Luo*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: When LLM-powered agents interact, information is repeatedly shared, expanded, and forgotten, yet multi-agent evaluation typically focuses on final task outcomes. We study such interaction as claim-level information dynamics and propose RIVER, which decomposes agent outputs into atomic claims and evaluates three complementary dimensions: convergence, exploration, and retention. Convergence captures cross-agent claim correspondence and stance alignment; exploration captures semantic breadth and extension beyond the source; and retention tracks source preservation and the persistence of new claims across stages and lineages. We evaluate five interaction strategies on 40 controlled news-generation cases. Repeatedly pooled interaction promotes convergence but narrows the information space, whereas distributed relay yields broader, less shared information. Further comparisons show that method-specific constraints increase exploration while weakening convergence and retention. Our findings suggest that collective information dynamics are better explained by underlying information-flow mechanisms than by simple downstream performance.

</details>


### [231] [Why Full-Duplex Speech Models Collapse in Long Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32349)
*Yeonju Kim,Bella Godiva,Se Jin Park,Yong Man Ro*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Full-duplex speech models listen and speak at the same time, which makes them promising for natural, open-ended conversation. But can they sustain a coherent conversation for tens of minutes? Because they generate a token every frame, even while listening, they exhaust their context windows within minutes. Yet existing benchmarks evaluate only single turns or a few conversational rounds, too short to expose the long-horizon problem. We introduce the Full-Duplex Long-Horizon Benchmark (FD-LHB), which tests five recent full-duplex models on long replayed conversations and in live conversation with an LLM partner. Every evaluated model eventually either degrades or exhausts its context. Models that keep the full history often degrade before their context runs out, while sliding-window models still fall into repetition, silence, and gibberish. For sliding-window models, we identify two contributing mechanisms. Gibberish and silence are linked to Initialization-Anchor Eviction, where the sliding window discards the first KV entry. Repetition is also reinforced by the model's own recent outputs. Guided by this diagnosis, we apply two simple fixes: Initialization-Anchor Pinning (IAP) keeps that first entry, and Duplex Repetition Penalty (DRP) discourages echoing while guarding against prolonged silence. Together, they support the diagnosis and, without retraining, substantially reduce conversational collapse in replays of up to an hour while running in real time.

</details>

Abstract: Spoken native language (L1) identification for second language learners has traditionally relied on acoustic attributes of speech. Comparatively little attention has been paid to analysing speech transcripts for L1 traces. In this paper, we present a study of L1 identification using both speech and corresponding transcripts from a spoken learner English corpus. We compare a WavLM-based audio L1 classifier with a zero-shot GPT-4o classifier operating on transcripts and analyse their predictions across proficiency levels using a CEFR-balanced evaluation set. Our experiments show that the audio classifier outperforms the transcript-based approach overall when full transcripts are available, although their relative strengths vary with learner proficiency. Audio performs better at A1, whereas text performs better from A2 to C1 with full transcripts. Transcript-based accuracy generally increases with proficiency, while acoustic performance remains comparatively stable. Restricting transcript length substantially reduces text-based accuracy, whereas audio is less sensitive to input length and performs relatively well with limited input. These findings provide insights into L1 identification from acoustic and textual representations of learner speech and how performance varies with proficiency and the amount of available language material.

</details>


### [233] [A Broader Look into MLLM's Retrieval Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32411)

Abstract: Locating relevant evidence in long inputs is a core capability of Multimodal Large Language Models. Prior studies attribute this capability to retrieval heads, a sparse set of attention heads that transfer information from the context to the output. However, these analyses are confined to a limited set of modalities and the corresponding models. Moreover, how retrieval heads can be leveraged to enhance the model calls for further exploration. In this paper, we ask whether this mechanism expands together with the input modalities of the model. Taking audio-visual input as a newly added modality, we construct paired inputs in which the same answer is either spoken or shown on screen. With the evidence content fixed, retrieval heads remain sparse in the new modality and are shared to a large extent across modalities. Across 18 language, vision-language, and omni-modal checkpoints, omni-modal models largely preserve the retrieval heads of their backbones. Ablating these heads eliminates nearly all audio-visual retrieval. The heads shared across modalities make up fewer than 2\% of all heads, yet ablating them also degrades document understanding, spatial perception, and visual mathematics. Building on these findings, we turn retrieval heads into a target of optimization by training their attention with a ranking objective. This raises the page Recall@1 of Qwen3-VL-8B on MMDocIR by 6.03 points and improves document retrieval across three model families. Functional attention heads can thus serve both as a lens on multimodal models and as a target for improving them.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based annotation has advanced rapidly, yet existing benchmarks remain misaligned with real-world practice. Actual annotation often involves lengthy, task-specific guidelines, and different annotators may produce divergent but reasonable judgments, especially on boundary cases, where single-labeled dataset fail to reflect this disagreement. This gap also limits existing single-agent and workflow-based annotation systems, which often lack adaptability to complex, evolving guidelines. To bridge this gap, we introduce ANT-A Benchmark, a real-world multi-annotator benchmark spanning 7 datasets across domains and modalities, with task-specific guidelines and 5 independent human labels per instance. ANT-A enables a distribution-aware evaluation, using Fleiss kappa to assess how closely model annotations align with human labeling distributions. We further propose ANT-OR, a multi-agent annotation framework that refines prompts by distilling contrastive decision rules from positive and negative feedback. Experiments show that ANT-OR improves agreement@>=2 accuracy by 4.5 points over the strongest baseline and achieves the highest alignment with human annotator distributions as measured by Fleiss kappa within 2 points. Code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/Krueske/ANT-OR.

</details>

Abstract: Building Task-Oriented Dialogue (TOD) systems for high-value scenarios such as financial marketing requires large volumes of domain-specific dialogue data, which privacy regulations, annotation costs, and the need for expert labor make hard to obtain. User simulation offers a remedy, but existing methods target general-domain dialogue and exhibit two key limitations: insufficient modeling of domain-specific business processes and compliance constraints, and inadequate capture of the colloquial, spoken-language character of voice interactions. We propose ProfileSim, a profile-driven user simulation framework for financial marketing outbound calls. It jointly models user profiles---demographics and Big Five personality traits---and business profiles---intents reverse-engineered from real call logs---enabling fine-grained control over simulated behavior. The base model is fine-tuned on real voice dialogue data and refined by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with hierarchical rewards at the dialogue and utterance levels. Paired with a customer service agent integrating Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) constraints and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), ProfileSim achieves the highest colloquial naturalness, the strongest user profile adherence and the lowest agent score on long-tail intents among all evaluated user generators, while producing substantially shorter user utterances at comparable business compliance. The generated dialogue data is designed to support intent recognition training, agent evaluation, and rapid cold-start deployment.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly capable of supporting scientific workflows, but their capabilities across diverse research activities remain difficult to assess systematically. Existing benchmarks often focus on selected scientific capabilities or task types, limiting a comprehensive characterization of scientific agents' strengths and weaknesses in realistic research workflows. We introduce SCI-AGENT-EXAM, a workflow-oriented benchmark comprising 120 expert-curated tasks across 11 scientific fields and 7 research task types defined by the Scientific Workflow Task Taxonomy (SWTT). Each task requires both executable artifacts (e.g., code and scripts) and textual artifacts (e.g., reports and explanations). To evaluate the heterogeneous task outputs, we develop a hybrid evaluation protocol combining deterministic programmatic verification for executable artifacts with a calibrated, expert-validated LLM-based judge for textual artifacts. Beyond outcome assessment, we analyze progress and solution stability along execution trajectories to characterize how agents reach their final results. Experiments with 10 frontier LLM agents reveal substantial performance gaps: GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest overall score of 80.9, yet only 26.0% of its runs exceed the 0.95 success threshold. Further analyses uncover distinct capability profiles across scientific task types and substantial variation in execution efficiency and stability. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/SKYLENAGE-AI/Sci-Agent-Exam.

</details>

Abstract: Pre-consultation medical dialogues are essential for collecting comprehensive patient information, but are costly to obtain at scale due to privacy and ethical constraints. Existing LLM-based synthesis methods often rely on prompt engineering or rigid rules, limiting their ability to balance clinical relevance with realistic patient diversity. We propose CoRPS-Dial, a controllable dual-agent framework for pre-consultation medical dialogue synthesis that integrates patient personas with guideline-aligned clinical workflow rules. CoRPS-Dial generates dialogues through interactions between a persona-driven patient agent and a process-guided doctor agent, followed by post-hoc quality control for role consistency, workflow completeness, and textual consistency. Experiments on coronary heart disease demonstrate that CoRPS-Dial improves alignment between synthetic and real dialogues in information coverage, clinical compliance, and dialogue naturalness. Downstream fine-tuning experiments further confirm the utility of the generated synthetic data for medical dialogue tasks. These results highlight the effectiveness of CoRPS-Dial for clinically grounded and controllable medical dialogue synthesis.

Abstract: While large vision-language models excel at optical character recognition, it remains unclear how their underlying vision encoders process text and its meaning, and whether they support anything resembling reading comprehension. We systematically study transformer-based vision encoders, using cross-modal alignment between rendered text and visual concepts together with probing and retrieval-based analyses as a controlled window into these broader capabilities. Our layer-wise analyses span orthographic, grammatical, and semantic organization, and reveal that strong cross-modal mapping emerges only in the deepest layers, after unimodal object and text recognition have already developed, coinciding with a progression from surface-level to semantic representations. Although this hierarchy is strongest in contrastively trained models, larger self-supervised models and text-rich training data partially support similar behaviors without explicit language supervision. Together, our results suggest that increasing model and training-data scale, as well as text-rich training data, can partially compensate for the absence of language supervision.

</details>

Abstract: Resources for toxicity detection in Punjabi remain limited, particularly for musical content that combines linguistic and multimodal information. We present the Punjabi Geet Toxicity Dataset (PGT-FG), a segment-level resource developed to support research on toxicity detection in Punjabi musical content. The dataset was constructed from publicly available Punjabi songs collected from YouTube. An initial collection of 620 songs resulted in 465 successfully downloaded songs and 3,402 preliminary segments from 450 songs. After quality control and annotation, the final dataset contains 2,396 unique segments from 392 songs. Four native Punjabi annotators produced 2,870 annotation records, including a shared subset of 158 segments annotated by all four. Each annotation includes binary toxicity, eight fine-grained toxicity categories, severity, sentiment, mode, and an uncertainty flag. Inter-annotator agreement was measured with Fleiss' kappa on the shared subset, and majority-based resolution produced the final segment-level labels. Of the 2,396 segments, 705 (29.42%) were labelled toxic and 1,691 (70.58%) non-toxic. Violence was the most frequent category, occurring in 539 toxic segments (76.45%), followed by gang references (227, 32.20%) and drug references (133, 18.87%). The overall toxicity label achieved substantial agreement (kappa = 0.6521). PGT-FG provides a fine-grained resource for Punjabi toxicity detection and multimodal content analysis. The resolved dataset, with one final record per segment, is publicly available on Zenodo (https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23040156). Original lyrics, lyric excerpts, and copyrighted audio or video content are not redistributed.

</details>

Abstract: This paper examines speech characteristics of the Austrian National Council members (CMs). We introduce analysis methodology based on a fine-tuned BERT-Model which assigns to every speech a probability distribution over Austrian parties. These are then aggregated at the speaker and party level. We perform an extensive evaluation to ensure the quality of the generated distributions prior to further analysis. Instead of considering mis-assignments of predicted parties as a performance issue, we leverage them to derive insights about the CMs and political developments. Our findings show that parties that govern together become more similar in their speech characteristics, while, analogously, parties that are in opposition move further away from governing parties. Moreover, CMs who switch party usually adapt their speech characteristics only after the switch occurs. Finally, we publicly release a data set containing party affiliation predictions for the speeches of the Austrian National Council from 1996 to 2018.

</details>

Abstract: Fine-tuning the non-blank predictor of a factorized transducer on target-domain text improves recognition on that domain but degrades performance on the source domain. A single interpolation weight between base and adapted predictors cannot resolve this: the adapted predictor helps at some prefixes and hurts at others, often within one utterance. We propose conservative residual routing: the adapted predictor becomes a residual correction to the base, and a 16k-parameter gate decides at each prefix how much of it to admit. Closing the gate reproduces the base exactly. On a 40-hour conversational corpus, routing retains essentially all of the text-only gain on two held-out domains (19.89 to 16.74, 20.91 to 16.21 WER) while leaving source-domain WER unchanged (12.90 to 12.93); the adapted predictor alone costs 1.08 WER. Routing needs no per-domain tuning on target-domain audio. Analysis reveals the gate does not behave as a simple domain-level switch: it modulates intervention through the disagreement between the two predictors, suppressing it in-domain and amplifying it out-of-domain.

</details>

Abstract: Adapting ASR to a domain with only untranscribed audio typically relies on pseudo-labeling, a strong teacher transcribes the audio and a student is fine-tuned on it. Because supervised fine-tuning (SFT) treats every pseudo-label token as a target, the student inherits the teacher's errors. We show that using the same pseudo-labels as a reward reference for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is more robust to label noise. In the banking domain, GRPO yields 29.3% to 56.2% relative WER improvements over SFT on Whisper-generated labels, and the advantage holds across four Whisper sizes, four teacher families, three domains and four languages. While SFT reproduces almost 100% of teacher errors verbatim, GRPO passes through only 27%. Rollout statistics explain why, erroneous pseudo-label words rarely appear in on-policy rollouts, so group-relative advantages stay aligned with the clean transcript, whereas SFT forces them into the policy's support and collapses the reward variance GRPO relies on.

</details>


### [244] [Natural Language Autoencoders Identify Without Explaining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32691)
*Kristina Šekrst,Barbara Geld*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Natural Language Autoencoders (NLAs) express a model's internal activations in the form of readable text. The verbalizer turns an activation into an English explanation; the reconstructor then converts that explanation back into an activation, and the explanation is considered successful if the round trip brings back the original activation. We claim that this criterion evaluates whether the information is sufficient and does not address descriptive faithfulness. A sentence can contain sufficient information to make it possible to identify an activation even if it wrongly explains what the activation does: in the case of reconstruction, the primary aim is to accurately identify the activations and not to describe them. This issue has the same structure as the chain-of-thought faithfulness problem, in which verbal output that has been trained to go with a process is taken as a report on that process, and it parallels the dissociation between an individual's introspective report and the actual governing process as documented in human psychology. Although proposed as interpretability tools for the purpose of alignment, NLAs reintroduce, through unverified explanations, the epistemic problem that they were intended to resolve.

</details>


### [245] [When is more search useful? An Empirical Study of Search Utility in Quality-Aware Decoding for Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32679)
*Javier Garcia Gilabert,Rico Sennrich,André F. T. Martins*

Abstract: Quality-Aware Decoding (QAD) improves machine translation through test-time scaling, generating multiple candidate translations and selecting the final output with a quality-aware decoder. Usually the same candidate budget N is spent on every source sentence, assuming that all inputs require the same amount of search. We show that this assumption does not hold and that the quality gain obtained from additional candidates varies across sentences. We study search utility through three quantities: total scaling gain, how much quality a sentence can gain from a much larger candidate pool; marginal scaling gain, the expected gain from generating one additional batch of candidates; and the sampling saturation point, the candidate budget required to realize most of the total scaling gain. We investigate two sources of information for predicting these quantities: signals available before candidate-set expansion, and statistics computed from the candidate pool accumulated during search. We find that sentence-level signals correlate with total scaling gain but are less informative about the sampling saturation point. In contrast, properties of the current candidate pool provide useful signals of marginal scaling gain. Finally, we show that these signals can identify sentences that obtain larger gains when additional search is selectively applied.

</details>

Abstract: Full-duplex speech models can listen and speak at the same time, enabling natural, low-latency interaction. In counseling, however, these models often focus on the latest utterance and lose track of the seeker's underlying problem. We call this failure Stressor Blindness. We propose CounselPlex, a stressor-aware full-duplex framework that uses two operations. CounselPlex updates a short stressor state during seeker silence and injects this state into <PAD> positions while listening. We also introduce RoPE Position Shift Augmentation (RPSA), which broadens positional coverage during training and improves performance at longer turn depths in our evaluation. LLM-judge evaluations show improved counseling quality and stressor grounding over strong baselines.

</details>

Abstract: The rapid expansion of scientific literature far outpaces the speed of manual curation, creating a fundamental bottleneck in extracting structured knowledge from scientific texts. To address this, we introduce an agentic, ontology-guided framework for knowledge graph (KG) construction. Our framework utilizes specialized agents for document understanding, information extraction, ontology conformance, and entity linking. First, an LLM extracts factual knowledge in the form of triples with explicit textual evidence from research papers. Then the triplets undergo a multistage verification process to ground the triplets in the ontology. By enforcing schema constraints and requiring evidence in the form of text fragments, we ensure the data is both semantically consistent and traceable. In an agreement study over 1,000 extracted triplets, Gemini-2.5-flash achieves Cohen's $κ$ scores of 0.640--0.700 against three human annotators, matching the human--human agreement range of 0.616--0.689. We applied this framework to 2,000 scientific publications and construct a novel, ontology-grounded KG for climate science with 133,990 nodes and 755,308 relations; of the 23,327 triplets that the publications contribute after validation, 98.96\% are judged correct. The pipeline code and KG is publicly available https://github.com/aayushacharya/climatekg.

</details>

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation enables post-training foundation models without relying on stronger external teachers. However, existing approaches rely on ground-truth question-answer pairs, human-curated problem sets, or other supervised signals. In this work, we reformulate on-policy self-distillation as a self-training framework, driven by self-generated question-answer pairs and privileged reasoning demonstrations. We push supervision to the limit: guided solely by coarse, high-level topic descriptions, the model synthesizes its own curriculum of queries and privileged reasoning traces without access to any human-curated prompts or answers. We find that self-distillation on fully synthetic privileged information enables a model to improve across multiple domains while retaining its capabilities on domains it is not self-trained on. We demonstrate the generality of this framework across text and multimodal domains, achieving consistent self-improvement on standard language benchmarks and Visual Question Answering. Our findings demonstrate that foundation models possess rich latent capabilities that can be amplified without external task supervision or curated problem distributions.

</details>

Abstract: Hospitals regularly buy software that clinicians and patients depend on. Because these purchases are costly, hard to reverse and can affect patient care, buyers look for evidence of which peer institutions already use a product and which systems it connects to. This evidence is public but scattered across vendor websites, press releases and third-party pages. Language-model agents can now collect it at low cost, but the correctness of the extracted claims is difficult to establish, and a wrongly listed customer can mislead buyers and misrepresent an organisation. We present a pipeline that builds a knowledge graph of customer and integration relations from the open web under one rule: every edge must be supported by a verbatim quote from its source page. Pages come from a deterministic crawl of each vendor's site and a budgeted ReAct agent that searches the rest of the web. A permissive extractor proposes relations with quotes, and a layered grounding stage decides which to keep, using a referent step that reads the whole page when a quote names only one side, deterministic checks, and a panel of five model calls voting on nine criteria. On 61 vendors, the pipeline reaches 0.94 precision and 0.71 recall against a page-level key covering the 6,428 candidate relations proposed by its extractor, whose verdicts were drafted by one model and re-checked with a second. Against the golden reference, the marketplace's own curated list, it scores lower, but 89% of the relations it keeps that the reference lacks are stated on a page. A confidence score built from the panel's votes keeps more correct relations than the fixed rule at the same precision, and reaches 0.90 precision at 0.90 recall. We also discuss how narrow, bounded model calls, string-matched evidence and voting address long-context degradation and run-to-run variance.

</details>

Abstract: On-policy distillation aligns teacher feedback with a student's current behavior, yet acquiring useful supervision for multi-turn agents requires identifying both where guidance is needed and whether the student can sustain the interaction afterward. We introduce Frontier-OPD, which addresses this problem through the student's learning frontier: decision situations that remain challenging but admit useful autonomous continuation after brief teacher guidance. To operationalize this frontier, Frontier-OPD organizes on-policy experience into a shared-state trajectory graph, where trajectory-balanced aggregation of teacher-student disagreement identifies distinct intervention candidates while preserving the histories needed for replay. Short teacher bridges are then assessed by comparing agreement over the bridge and the ensuing student continuation with an equally long segment of the original trajectory, providing a behavioral proxy for guidance that the current student can build on. Accepted teacher responses augment ordinary distillation data, and fresh rollouts renew the frontier estimate as the policy changes. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld report higher mean autonomous performance than the compared distillation baselines across the evaluated student scales and teacher configurations, with ablations indicating complementary effects of candidate selection and guidance filtering. In one ALFWorld timing setting, the additional collection cost is offset by a shorter time to a target success rate.

</details>

Abstract: For agricultural advisory, generating an answer is only part of the problem: the system must also control unsafe requests, work with limited connectivity, and accommodate changing guidance. KRISHOKTECH is a Bengali agricultural advisory system built around an explicit authorization boundary around generation. Before retrieval, normalization and rule-based admission checks refer policy-covered high-risk requests and ask for a crop when treatment intent is underspecified. The system then binds retrieval to a single crop and asks for confirmation when the text and image indicate different crops. After generation, a narrow verifier removes dosage claims not supported by retrieved BARI/BRRI evidence. Agricultural guidance is kept in an external evidence index that can be updated without retraining the generator, while browser-side vision and local retrieval reduce connectivity requirements. The interface makes these decisions visible as ASK, CONFIRM, REFER, and DROP, together with their evidence. On 200 farmer queries and 400 stress queries, the controls halted underspecified requests and reduced cross-crop retrieval violations from 36.5% to 0% under the tested alias filter. Mismatch detection caught 453 of 454 text-image conflicts, while the verifier caught 114 of 118 inserted dosage errors. The demonstration shows how these controls govern the path from request to delivered advice.

</details>

Abstract: Latent communication requires both an effective continuous reasoning process and an interface through which an external model can leverage its intermediate computation. We present J-Comm, a framework comprising two core components. First, a training-free Geometric Agreement for Latent Reasoning (GEAR) reweights token candidates over existing model embeddings, which monotonically optimizes geometric consensus among candidate tokens in embedding space at each step. Second, we introduce an H+J (Hidden-and-Jacobian) communication interface that gives the Receiver two complementary views of the Sender's reasoning. The H channel supplies continuous inputs derived from feedback distributions, while the J channel conveys intermediate-state cues in vocabulary-indexed Jacobian-lens coordinates. Intuitively, H provides inputs for the Receiver to reason from, while J offers additional guidance within its computation. The Receiver reconstructs these cues using its own dictionary and injects them into intermediate layers during prefill, enabling communication without sharing key-value caches or requiring identical model widths. In Qwen3-8B to Qwen3-4B communication, J-Comm achieves the highest mean score across seven benchmarks, including 94.80% on MATH500, 54.55% on GPQA-Diamond, 73.33% on AIME2024+2025, and 62.93% success on ALFWorld. Compared with TextMAS, it improves performance by 1.29-8.59 percentage points and reduces generated text tokens by 19.2-74.8%.

</details>

Abstract: Modern language models rely on subwording tokenization to define the discrete units of input processing, typically using methods like Byte Pair Encoding (BPE) that treat whitespace as a fixed semantic boundary. This design is inherently limited, as many meaningful concepts---including multiword expressions and the structured environments of formal languages like LaTeX---frequently span across whitespaces. While recent superword tokenization methods attempt to address this by relaxing whitespace constraints, they often rely on greedy frequency-driven merging that fails to account for token length or the specific needs of underrepresented domain-specific languages. In this paper, we introduce Adaptive Frequency Threshold Byte Pair Encoding (AdaBPE), a new tokenization method that leverages an adaptive frequent-pattern mining approach. AdaBPE features a twofold dynamicity: it adapts minimum frequency thresholds based on both the length of a candidate token, to prevent favoring frequent short patterns, and its type, to distinguish between general and formal language patterns, using LaTeX as case in point. This allows the model to efficiently allocate vocabulary capacity to domain-specific notations, such as LaTeX, even when they represent a small fraction of the training data. We evaluate AdaBPE across five datasets spanning LaTeX and natural language, showing consistent gains in encoding efficiency and downstream language-model performance on domain-specific content without compromising general-language performance.

</details>

Abstract: Despite advances in multilingual text representations, effective dense retrieval for Persian remains constrained by the limited availability of language-specific training resources and compact embedding models. We present Yalda Embedding, a 595.8-million-parameter Persian retrieval model adapted from Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B using more than one million query-document pairs. On eight Persian retrieval tasks from FaMTEB, the model achieves the highest macro-average among the evaluated 0.6B models, scoring 0.5488 and outperforming Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B and F2LLM-v2-0.6B on every task. It also approaches Qwen3-Embedding-4B, winning on four tasks, tying on one, and trailing by 0.0075 in macro-average. An incremental ablation study examines contrastive batch size, optimization stabilization, and relational knowledge distillation from F2LLM-v2-8B. Under the one-epoch training setup, contrastive fine-tuning alone yields a modest mean improvement over initialization and regressions on two tasks. The final distilled configuration improves all eight tasks over the non-distilled model, raising the mean from 0.4813 to 0.5488 without increasing inference-time model size. These gains include hard-negative evaluation tasks despite training without explicitly mined or supplied hard negatives. We release model weights, training and evaluation code, the pairwise dataset, and its teacher-embedded counterpart, enabling inspection of data composition and training procedures, independent evaluation, and reuse without repeating expensive teacher inference.

</details>

Abstract: Personal email agents decide, for every message, what it is, whether it is urgent, whether to draft a reply, create a task, or ignore it. Their error rate is rarely measured: the stored labels were written by the system itself. We present Foyer, a self-hosted personal inbox agent in daily use, and a measurement protocol (stratified sampling, frozen snapshots, thread-grouped splits, pre-registered hypotheses, human labels in the agent's review loop) that yields ground truth before any training. On 739 real Italian/English emails labelled by the owner (231 unbiased, 500 adversarially sampled), we compare nine systems under thread-grouped cross-validation: the production keyword rules, a TF-IDF + logistic-regression baseline, local typed decision models (Tev1, Nimble), a hosted decision model (Nimble v4-max), and hosted generative LLMs (Qwen2.5-7B, Gemma 4 26B/31B). Four results. The production rules score 28% on message type against a 58% majority class: the field shown most prominently carried no information. With ~200 labels, logistic regression has the highest accuracy on every field (type 0.79, urgency 0.74, needs-reply 0.83, is-noise 0.90), though it never predicts the two rarest classes and is statistically tied with Gemma 4 on type; few-shot prompting does not close the gap. The sender's relationship (client, supplier, service) is recovered from other emails of the same contact with 98.6% accuracy where the contact has been seen, and is not inferable from a single message by any model: it belongs in the contact graph, not the prompt. Finally, accuracy is not the product metric: composed into actions, the most accurate system most often ignores emails needing action (18% vs 4% for the best LLM); a cost-chosen threshold on its noise probability halves that rate and makes it the cheapest system.

</details>

### [259] [See What I Need, Dream What I Want: A Navigation Foundation Model on a Self-Constructed Representation Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31762)
*Jiawei Chen,Yinxi Wang,Zihan Yang,Meijun Gao,Yaowei Liu,Shuming Zhang,Zecheng Li,Xiangju Mi,Yuxuan Liu,Runzhong Huang,Tianlin Li,Xun Zhang,Zhengyang Wang,Xu Miao,Tong Yang*

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: What visual representation should a navigation policy act on? Navigation policies built on vision foundation models largely leave representation design to the encoders' pretraining objectives. We present RepresentNav, a navigation foundation model built on a self-constructed representation space: a visual representation constructed by the policy's own objectives from vision foundation models. RepresentNav builds this space with Representation Residuals, a compact three-layer transformer that attends over features spanning the full depth of multiple complementary vision foundation models, so that the policy learns to see what it needs for navigation. In the same space it dreams what it wants: before every action a latent chain-of-thought predicts the representation of the scene a moment ahead. The dream is trained end-to-end with the policy as a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), with a next-representation prediction loss and a regularizer on its targets. RepresentNav sets a new state of the art on VLNVerse, VLN-CE R2R, EVT-Bench, and two point-goal benchmarks, and its gains carry over to a physical robot amid clutter and moving people. On VLNVerse fine-grained, RepresentNav achieves 83.80% success, outperforming the previous state of the art by 20.05 percentage points, and on EVT-Bench distracted tracking it reaches 85.5% with a single camera (+10.1). With the same encoders, the self-constructed space clearly outperforms concatenating their outputs and learns to weight their layers unevenly, and a dream in it cuts collisions by a quarter. A single dreamed latent decodes into the depth, semantics, and 3D occupancy of the scene a moment ahead, more accurately than pretrained encoders applied to the real future frame, although occupancy is never supervised, and the rollout tracks the robot's own motion over the next second: the dream emerges as a compact latent world model.

</details>

Abstract: The life-cycle of robotics foundation models crucially depends on data labels. Outcome reward labels are needed for monitoring real-world deployments. Process reward labels are needed during RL post-training. Timestamped captions are needed for filtering and balancing pretraining data. We present Flora, an industrial-quality data engine used for training GR00T models, that simultaneously covers these capabilities. Flora achieves SOTA scores on the captioning benchmark WGO-Bench and reward benchmarks RoboReward and Robometer. On internal benchmarks it is competitive with GPT-6 Astra while beating other frontier models. The model is substantially cheaper than API-based alternatives, roughly 125 times cheaper than GPT-6 Astra. Florais battle tested at scale, labeling 200,000 hours of data per day across 512 GB300s. By open-sourcing our data engine, we enable researchers to scale custom robotics datasets and explore emerging applications such as physical auto-research and human-to-robot transfer.

</details>

Abstract: Segmenting robot demonstration data into disjoint temporal and semantic subsegments is valuable for learning, cleaning, retrieval, and analysis. Segmentation is often performed manually in a tedious and error prone process. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have the potential for automating this process, but directly ingesting raw multi camera video streams into MLLMs can be costly and can quickly exhaust context windows. In this paper, we introduce KiRoSe (Kinematics for Robot Segmentation), a novel framework that focuses on the companion stream of robot kinematic data. Inspired by the principle of classical entropy coding, such as Huffman coding, where frequently occurring symbols are represented more compactly, we propose Sparse Motion Tokens (SMTs), a discrete kinematic vocabulary designed to compactly represent recurring, temporally localized motion patterns, which we refer to as kinematic events. SMTs are learned by encoding kinematic trajectories with temporal segment supervision and quantizing the resulting features. We use increasingly available robot demonstration subtask annotations to supervise an open language model to interpret the resulting token sequences. Evaluation against human labeled segmentation shows that KiRoSe can substantially outperform baseline segmentation methods in F1 quality and transfers zero shot to unseen robot embodiments and egocentric human motion, while preserving the language models open vocabulary knowledge. Beyond segmentation, KiRoSe tracks task progress on an unseen robot and detects when an action is being reversed.(website: https://berkeleyautomation.github.io/kirose/)

</details>

Abstract: Preference-based reinforcement learning (PbRL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for learning robot behaviors aligned with human intent. However, its scalability is fundamentally limited by the need to collect expensive human preference labels for every new task. In this work, we present PrefO2O, an offline-to-online latent preference transfer framework that enables zero-shot PbRL on unseen target tasks without requiring any human feedback. The core idea is to learn a task-invariant latent preference space from a small amount of offline preference data on source tasks, where transferable preference structures are disentangled from task-specific variations. This learned manifold allows PrefO2O to transfer preference structures and reward priors to new tasks under distribution shift, while confidence-weighted optimal transport alignment generates reliable pseudo-preference supervision for online reward adaptation. Extensive experiments on robotic manipulation and locomotion benchmarks demonstrate that PrefO2O substantially outperforms existing preference-transfer baselines and matches or exceeds PbRL methods trained with real human feedback, while generalizing across multi-to-one and one-to-one transfer settings as well as across different robot embodiments.

### [263] [SEVI: Safe Evidential Real-World Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31829)

Abstract: Real-world reinforcement learning has enabled contact-rich manipulation tasks to be trained directly on physical robots, but unsafe interactions during online learning remain a key barrier to practical deployment. We propose Safe Evidential Real-World Reinforcement Learning (SEVI), a framework for reliability-aware safety intervention during real-world policy learning. SEVI introduces an Evidential Safety Critic that estimates both future constraint-violation risk and epistemic uncertainty in a single forward pass. During online learning, SEVI evaluates each task-policy action before execution; if the action is predicted to be unsafe or the prediction is insufficiently supported by evidence, SEVI overrides it with an offline-trained recovery policy that guides the robot toward a lower-risk region. We evaluate SEVI on two simulated manipulation tasks and a real-world USB insertion task under in-distribution and distribution-shifted conditions. Across task-learning and safety-critic baselines, SEVI achieves the strongest trajectory-level safety prediction, the highest final task success, and the lowest unsafe-event counts. SEVI also reduces unsafe events by 13.4 to 57.1 percent relative to recovery-based safe RL while preserving low-latency execution-time filtering. The project page is available at: https://sevi-safe.github.io/

</details>


### [264] [WorldMirror: Agentic Real-to-Sim Generation of Interactive Worlds from In-the-Wild Robot Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31834)

Abstract: Existing approaches for robot learning in simulation often require substantial manual effort to construct scenes and specify tasks, limiting training scalability and data diversity. Meanwhile, in-the-wild robot demonstrations naturally provide diverse and realistic observations of real-world interactions. Leveraging these demonstrations for simulation requires both high-fidelity visual reconstruction and physically consistent interactions. General-purpose agents offer flexible adaptation to diverse scenarios and strong reasoning about 3D spatial relationships, while specialized generative models provide high-fidelity reconstruction of scene appearance and geometry. We introduce WorldMirror, an agentic real-to-sim framework that constructs interactive digital twins from in-the-wild robot demonstrations. Our central idea is to formulate real-to-sim construction as the iterative refinement of an executable world program. A coding agent decomposes the observed scene, plans reconstruction steps, and orchestrates specialized image and 3D generation tools, refining the program through verification feedback. A camera-pose-anchored scene representation organizes the world into static visual context, support geometry, and editable interactive assets while preserving spatial relationships among the scene, objects, and robot. The agent uses this representation to diagnose and correct reconstruction errors, enabling physically consistent interaction and trajectory replay. Experiments on DROID demonstrate that WorldMirror can reconstruct visually faithful and geometrically consistent interactive worlds while preserving the physical validity of recorded robot trajectories.

</details>

Abstract: Coding agents can translate language instructions into robot programs. For contact-rich manipulation, a key challenge is to generate and refine control logic that responds to physical interaction. We present FORCA, a coding agent that takes visual observations and wrist force/torque (F/T) measurements as input to write Python programs for contact probing and force-responsive motion. Between executions, the agent inspects force records alongside visual outcomes to diagnose failures and revise both motion and force-feedback control logic. FORCA requires no demonstrations, task-specific training, or simulation. Across five real-robot tasks, we demonstrate its effectiveness in three forms of contact-rich manipulation: improving manipulation accuracy, adapting to changes in object or surface properties, and regulating contact under surface variation and object motion. Compared with a vision-only variant of the same agent, FORCA improves grasp accuracy and task success and reduces contact loss in wiping and peeling.

</details>

Abstract: Scaling policy learning requires broad experience and reliable supervision. Robot demonstrations provide high-quality trajectory supervision but are costly to collect. Egocentric videos broaden task and environment coverage, yet motion reconstruction and retargeting introduce noisy action labels. To address this challenge, we introduce EgoForesight, a vision-language-action framework that learns jointly from both sources by reducing the influence of unreliable action labels and exploiting demonstrated outcomes as complementary supervision. A joint vision-action flow couples future visual representation prediction with action generation, unifying reliability-aware action learning with outcome-informed representation transfer. Specifically, reliability-conditioned corruption allocates action supervision across noise levels according to source reliability, while reliability-weighted regression regulates each source's contribution. Complementing this treatment of noisy labels, privileged-future representation distillation draws supervision from demonstrated outcomes. An exponential-moving-average teacher observes the current scene and clean future to encode action-relevant structure from the resulting scene change. Its deeper representations supervise an earlier student layer receiving a corrupted future, providing outcome-informed structure beyond noisy action annotations. Together, these mechanisms support effective scaling with noisy egocentric actions. Over the tested range, EgoForesight exhibits steeper scaling slopes than the baseline, reducing real-world open-loop action MSE by 20.5% and improving RoboCasa few-shot success by 8.1 percentage points at 30,000 hours of pretraining data. After post-training, it also achieves strong absolute performance in both simulated and real-world dexterous manipulation.

</details>

Abstract: Dynamic manipulation of deformable linear objects (DLOs) remains challenging due to underactuated, high-dimensional dynamics, delayed deformation responses, and the presence of multiple feasible motions under obstacle constraints. This paper presents BM2PC, a Bayesian Multimodal MPC framework for agile manipulation of DLOs. Building on the control-as-inference posterior, BM2PC formulates multimodal planning as a set optimization problem that balances control quality and behavioral diversity. An efficient incremental update maintains multiple promising control modes with low additional computational overhead. For real-world deployment, BM2PC incorporates an execution dynamics model that predicts actual end-effector motion and compensates for execution mismatch during fast manipulation. We evaluate BM2PC on rope tracking and obstacle avoidance in simulation and hardware, and on simulated disturbance rejection. In trajectory tracking, BM2PC generalizes better to unseen trajectories than a learning-based baseline without task-specific policy training or fine-tuning. Real-world experiments demonstrate accurate long-horizon tracking, reducing RMSE by 44.0% on the longest task. In obstacle avoidance, BM2PC achieves comparable performance to existing multimodal MPC methods while reducing planning time by 29% to 66%. These results demonstrate accurate dynamic control and efficient multimodal planning within a unified MPC framework. The core implementation of BM2PC and supplementary videos are available at https://github.com/Longyouyuan/BM2PC.

</details>

Abstract: When a vision-language-action (VLA) model is fine-tuned with its trainable weights in bfloat16 (bf16) and no fp32 master copy, AdamW writes each update straight into bf16, silently rounding away any update smaller than 1/512 of a weight. In 9 of 12 open VLA codebases a default or documented recipe trains this way, 4 on full weight matrices, including LeRobot's SmolVLA default and its $π_0$/$π_{0.5}$ guides. In our LIBERO runs without the copy, a probe every 100 steps finds at least 79% of updates to bf16 weights erased. On the Hugging Face Hub, 99% of sampled full-weight SmolVLA fine-tunes store the action expert in bf16, and 84% of recent $π_{0.5}$ fine-tunes with a training configuration set the bf16 flag. On LIBERO-Object, with rules fixed in advance, an fp32 copy raises success over LeRobot's storage from 4.3% to 49.3% for SmolVLA and from 5.1% to 80.9% for $π_{0.5}$ at a ten-times lower learning rate, where both models are undertrained, and from 89.0% to 97.7% for $π_{0.5}$ at its default rate. For SmolVLA at its default rate it adds 10.2 points with open-loop 50-action chunks but only 4.2 when re-planning every 10 actions. In these five comparisons and two more against SmolVLA with every trainable weight in bf16, every run with the copy beats every run without it (p < 0.05); all gaps but the 8.7- and 4.2-point ones clear our 10-point threshold. The fix is to keep the trainable weights in fp32 during training, a one-line change for SmolVLA in LeRobot.

</details>


### [269] [Emergent Cooperative Thermoregulation in Lunar Swarm Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31868)
