Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Swarms of small robots are an attractive way to survey lunar caves and lava tubes. Missions such as NASA's CADRE will fly cooperating rovers for distributed mapping, but only in daylight. The 354 h lunar night drops the surface towards 95 K, and the radioisotope heaters that let single rovers survive it cannot be supplied at swarm scale. In this study we investigate whether a swarm can instead use its own members as thermal shielding. We train one policy, shared by all twenty robots, by reinforcement learning with an evolution-strategies estimator. The policy receives a single reward, once, at the end of the night: the mean temperature of the five coldest robots. Nothing in that reward refers to position, spacing, exposure or motion. Two behaviors nonetheless emerge. The swarm aggregates, and robots circulate between the sheltered core and the exposed rim, the same pair of behaviors with which emperor penguins survive the Antarctic winter. We measure the survival threshold, the smallest stored energy per robot at which every robot is still alive at sunrise. Across ten runs it is 155.6 +/- 1.3 pcm-h, where one pcm-h is 7.2 kJ of stored heat. That is 37% below a motionless huddle and within 13% of a physics-based lower bound, and at a budget where the motionless huddle loses six robots, every robot survives. The behavior needs no shared spatial reference and no robot-robot contact, and transfers from twenty robots to forty without retraining.

</details>

Abstract: Data-driven robots must learn to avoid collisions with as few demonstrations as possible to avoid collecting large datasets of destructive collision examples. We explore whole-body proximity sensors as inductive biases for learning reflexive collision-dodging behavior and find that lossy sensors with low resolution were stronger biases than high-resolution coverage. We also found that, perhaps unintuitively, anticipating when and where contact happens on the body of a robot reduced its learning sample efficiency. We investigated eight candidate representations in a reinforcement learning environment, which included latent compression, future contact anticipation, lossy corruption, and privileged state estimation. The candidate representations were tested by training over 200 humanoid robots to play a simplified version of dodgeball end-to-end under an intentionally constrained sample and compute budget.

</details>

Abstract: Swarm morphogenesis requires robots to assemble desired shapes through local interactions. We present a modular framework integrating neural cellular automata (NCA) with existing site selection and collision-aware control. Within this framework, we introduce a decentralized attachment-site discovery mechanism. Robots already in the formation update and share local NCA states to identify new attachment sites, enabling progressive growth without a predefined list of sites during deployment. To bridge the mismatch between conventional NCA growth and gradual physical assembly, we additionally propose offline sparse-retention fine-tuning that simulates partial attachment, together with a false-positive loss that penalizes invalid site activations. Simulations demonstrate formation of diverse lattice morphologies, scaling to several hundred robots, and substantial improvements in morphology accuracy from the proposed fine-tuning. Experiments with Crazyflie quadrotors validate the integrated execution pipeline using robot-relative observations reconstructed from external tracking.

</details>


### [272] [Silence Is Not Assurance: When Production Lane Keeping Assist Does Not Warn, and What It Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31899)

Abstract: Production lane keeping assist (LKA) systems tell the driver whether they detect the lane markings, but whether this displayed status reflects what the system perceives, and what happens when it does not, has not been examined in real-world driving. This study introduces CANDID-LKA, a CAN-based Diagnosis of the Indicated Detection status in LKA. It evaluates a proprietary driver-facing status using three signals: the displayed status and the steering torque of the driver, both recorded from the controller area network (CAN) bus of the vehicle, and the lane perception confidence of a third-party vision system observing the same road. The framework examines how the status responds to driving conditions and estimates how completely it reports degraded lane perception by comparing it with the other two signals in a latent-class model. We apply it to 39,800 seconds of real-world driving from 13 vehicle models of three manufacturers. The results show that the status does not respond consistently to the conditions that make markings harder to see, and that the three makes differ widely in how often they report not detected. The latent-class model shows that the displayed status is incomplete: it reports only 47% of degraded seconds as not detected, compared with 80% for the third-party low perception confidence, and in half of the low-confidence episodes it never reports not detected. In the silent seconds in which the status reads detected while the perception confidence is low and the driver is not in control, the standard deviation of lateral position over the next 3 s is about 60% larger than when the status and the perception confidence agree. A detected status thus does not guarantee reliable lane perception, and silence with the driver not in control is the case that most needs attention.

</details>

Abstract: In traditional Gaussian process (GP) regression, the inputs are typically assumed to be known, and uncertainty enters only through the measurements. When scalar fields are mapped using GP regression with measurements obtained from a mobile sensor, the inputs correspond to the estimated pose of the mobile sensor. The goal in this letter is to understand how a recorded GP, trained on erroneous inputs (e.g., pose estimates from dead-reckoning), can be corrected in a numerically efficient way when better estimates of the inputs become available. In the developed bias-aware GP (Ba-GP) framework, the recorded GP posterior-mean gradient is used to propagate estimated input bias and noise into mean and covariance corrections. The resulting additive covariance structure is then exploited to develop numerically efficient first-order correction algorithms. A simulation study and an indoor light-intensity mapping experiment show that Ba-GP improves predictive accuracy and uncertainty calibration relative to noisy-input GP baselines, with an order-of-magnitude reduction in computational cost relative to full GP retraining.

</details>

Abstract: Physical self-reproduction in modular robots remains difficult because a robot must not only move, but also collect, align, dock, and separate modules reliably in hardware. Taking inspiration from how genetically identical cells form the varied tissues of an organism, we introduce a single standardized robotic module that combines a magnetic docking interface with two actuated joints and folds from a flat, two-dimensional configuration into three-dimensional structures. Modules of this single design can be assembled into two morphologies: a three-module "trilobite" and a five-module "seal". Using a small set of discrete motion commands, a human operator steers an assembled parent robot to dock magnetically with a sequence of spare modules, which are assembled into a second, structurally identical individual that then separates from the parent. Because the parent retains all of its original modules, this is a budding-style form of physical reproduction. Here, we report preliminary hardware demonstrations of this full process on both morphologies. We discuss the current scope of the system, including its present reliance on direct human teleoperation rather than autonomous control, and outline directions toward autonomous execution of this process and repeated validation across a larger number of independent trials.

</details>

Abstract: Behavior cloning for robotic manipulation is highly sensitive to the coverage of its training data, yet collecting diverse successful demonstrations remains costly. We study reinforcement learning (RL) not as a deployable controller, but as a data generator for downstream visuomotor imitation learning. Our key premise is that multiple successful solution modes cover a broader range of successful trajectories than repeatedly collecting a single narrow solution. We introduce DiscoDemo, which trains a privileged latent-conditioned RL generator with a parallel reverse curriculum and a success-gated diversity objective to discover diverse solutions while preserving task success. The trained generator produces multiple successful closed-loop behaviors, which are safety-filtered and exported as a static dataset for downstream imitation learning. Across four manipulation tasks, DiscoDemo produces broader successful-state coverage and improves downstream visuomotor policies in both simulation and the real world. Averaged across tasks, downstream success increases to 62% from 40% for the strongest baseline in simulation, and to 78% from 39% on the real robot, respectively. These results suggest that successful solution diversity improves imitation learning for a pretrained flow-matching VLA and remains complementary to corrective data collection. Project page: https://davian-robotics.github.io/DiscoDemo

</details>

Abstract: Flow-matching Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models generate expressive continuous trajectories for robotic manipulation, yet balancing real-time responsiveness with execution stability remains a persistent challenge. Standard deployment workflows typically treat numerical solver depth and execution horizon as decoupled choices. The horizon amortizes inference latency, while solver depth is kept high to preserve integration fidelity. In this paper, we reveal a fundamental compute-execution coupling in flow-matching VLAs governed by a systematic sign reversal. While coarse numerical integration degrades precision under rapid replanning, it markedly improves task performance under extended open-loop commitment. Geometrically, numerical truncation contracts lateral action dispersion around an invariant trajectory centroid, serving as an implicit regularizer that suppresses compounding open-loop drift. Across LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world Franka manipulation on representative VLA backbones including $π_0$, $π_{0.5}$, and GR00T, exploiting this joint coupling consistently achieves superior task success while drastically lowering inference latency, entirely training-free and without architectural modifications. These findings establish numerical sampling depth as an operational variance regulator, shifting its role from a static precision budget to an active deployment knob in robot learning. Project page: https://jiabingyang01.github.io/Horizon-Depth-VLA/

</details>

Abstract: Traversability analysis in off-road settings remains a fundamental challenge for mobile robots. Key difficulties include constructing an accurate and expressive local map from multimodal sensor data, and using the map to design traversability rules that yield desirable navigation behavior. Importantly, this system must be resilient to the limited sensing regime brought about by complex environments and high speeds. In this paper, we present TravSUITE, a traversability system suitable for high-speed navigation in off-road environments. TravSUITE consists of two major components: 1) an efficient voxel mapper that leverages visual foundation models (VFMs) to build a rich geometric-semantic local map from streams of on-board sensor data, and 2) a unified neural network that jointly predicts traversability-relevant quantities in bird's eye view (BEV), including geometry, semantics, speed and cost. Our training strategy is entirely annotation-free and self-supervised, leveraging tasks such as map inpainting and inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) to learn both map representations and traversability. We perform a thorough ablation study and comparison to state-of-the-art approaches, and the results indicate that cost learning and auxiliary inpainting each contribute significantly to planning quality, and their combination is critical for achieving state-of-the-art performance in off-road path planning. We also design a simple risk adaptation mechanism to leverage our method's uncertainty estimates at deploy-time, and demonstrate that a combination of inpainting and risk estimation can result in 80% fewer navigation errors and 5% faster autonomous traversal speeds in real-world hardware experiments.

</details>

Abstract: Robot-assisted dressing is particularly challenging due to narrow openings and articulated body parts. Even small errors in the relative pose between the body part and garment opening can cause snagging, missed insertion, or incomplete dressing. To address these challenges, we formulate dressing as a geometry-aware insertion problem that represents task-relevant spatial relationships between the body part and object opening, rather than relying on the full deformable-object state. Based on this formulation, we propose a Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework combining a staged curriculum, which progressively guides the policy from coarse alignment to insertion and final placement, with a finger-wise insertion soft gate that provides continuous feedback on individual finger insertion progress and helps suppress premature insertion. We evaluate the framework on rigid and deformable bracelet dressing tasks through component ablations, systematic variations in the initial hand pose, and closed-loop execution of the learned policies on a real robotic system. On the post-training deformable evaluation, the full method reaches 84.7 +- 1.2% success. The insertion gate accounts for the largest gain, and adding the curriculum on top of that gate reaches the top score. On a mannequin hand the same full policy succeeds on 15/20 real-robot trials.

</details>

Abstract: Contact angle (CA) is a common parameter measured in the development of novel contact lens materials and surface coating technologies. Existing literature on in vitro CA of contact lenses via the sessile drop method is hindered in repeatability, reliability, and comparability due to inter-laboratory and inter-operator protocol variation. In this work, the Robotic Autonomous Imaging Surface Evaluator for vision applications (RAISE-Vision) is introduced as an automated platform for CA measurement on curved hydrogel surfaces, with specific application to in vitro contact lens wettability analysis throughout air exposure. Customized 3D printed hardware, such as lens mounts, camera holders and drying apparatuses, was designed to facilitate an automated repeated sessile drop workflow within a liquid handler. Collected images were directly fed to a custom open-source image processing script for CA determination on a curved surface that combines the elliptical fit and tangent methods to allow for variations in droplet shape. The CA of senofilcon A contact lenses measured using the RAISE-Vision platform increased from $62.6 \pm 5.5^\circ$ to $83.4 \pm 2.1^\circ$ over 10 sequential measurements in air and was able to replicate results obtained using an analogous manual protocol ($p = 0.470$). The custom image processing script was also separately demonstrated to generate comparable results to a commercially available CA determination software ($p = 0.343$). Aside from minimizing operator variability for CA measurement, RAISE-Vision provides a platform for standardized in vitro contact lens wettability analysis and understanding of surface wetting behaviour during air exposure.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as powerful general-purpose robot policies, but their successes and failures remain difficult to diagnose and understand. In this work, we show that sparse autoencoder (SAE) representations of OpenVLA activations contain outcome-predictive information and can be used to predict task failure before the robot reaches an unrecoverable state. Moreover, to provide insights into what common behaviors may contribute to these outcomes, we identify individual SAE features whose strong activations are associated with specific stages of success or failure, and uncover recurring behavioral patterns by localizing temporal intervals where these features exhibit significant activation changes. We further develop a VLM-based interpretation pipeline to generate behavioral descriptions of these patterns and validate their correspondence to feature activations on held-out intervals.

</details>


### [282] [RoboCode: Learning Reusable Action Primitives for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32225)

Abstract: Recent robot manipulation policies commonly predict continuous action chunks directly from observations, without explicitly modeling reusable structure in the action space. However, manipulation demonstrations often contain recurring short-horizon motion patterns that are shared across tasks. We introduce \textsc{RoboCode}, which learns these patterns through a shared soft-gated primitive action interface. Rather than assigning each action chunk to a single discrete code, \textsc{RoboCode} represents it as a soft composition of multiple learned primitives, providing a flexible intermediate representation of short-horizon actions. The primitive interface is learned from action chunks pooled across multiple tasks and then frozen while task-specific state encoders are trained to map current state inputs into the shared latent space.
  Experiments on five ManiSkill manipulation tasks address three questions. First, the learned primitive interface reconstructs action chunks with an average reconstruction MSE of 0.0187. Second, analysis of code activations reveals structured code reuse across tasks. Third, \textsc{RoboCode} achieves the highest or tied closed-loop success rate on all evaluated tasks, with an average success rate of 75.6%, compared with 68.8% for ACT and 41.6% for the larger Diffusion Policy baseline. These results suggest that soft-gated primitive codebooks provide an effective and interpretable intermediate action representation for robot manipulation.

</details>

Abstract: Protection levels (PLs) bound position errors under specified fault hypotheses and risk budgets, but advanced re-ceiver autonomous integrity monitoring (ARAIM) is formulated for snapshot GNSS estimation rather than the correlated poste-rior of tightly coupled GNSS/IMU factor graph optimization (FGO). This paper formulates ARAIM solution separation and PL computation on the FGO posterior. Because current-epoch pseudorange factors enter only the current-state block of the fixed-lag information matrix, the fault-excluded position covari-ance, solution separation, and nominal-bias propagation follow exactly from the linearized current-state marginal, which re-tains correlations with velocity, IMU-bias, and clock states and with historical information. Woodbury updates with selected-output evaluation compute these quantities for fault-free, single-satellite, constellation-wide, and second-order hypotheses with-out repeated full-state inversion or graph re-optimization. Sur-rounding monitoring layers screen measurements, handle post-fit outliers, and determine PL validity and availability. In two road experiments, both nominal sequences achieve 100% hori-zontal and vertical empirical bounding over PL-valid epochs, with a maximum error-to-PL ratio of 0.96. Normalized errors show that the posterior covariance alone does not capture slowly varying ranging biases: a covariance-only bound computed from the same posteriors retains no resolvable margin, and strong temporal correlation limits the empirical evidence to consisten-cy checks rather than validation of the integrity budget. On identical Experiment A posteriors, the proposed method reduces fault-hypothesis evaluation and PL solving time by 65.2% rela-tive to direct full-state inversion with matching integrity deci-sions, and controlled fault injections demonstrate fail-safe PL invalidation while navigation continues.

</details>

Abstract: Achieving accurate, real-time lane-keeping control during high-speed cornering on large-curvature roads while ensuring robust vehicle stability is a critical challenge in autonomous driving. To address this challenge, this study developed a robust offset-free model predictive control (OF-MPC) framework for vision-based autonomous vehicle lane-keeping. The regulation problem in this framework is solved using several algorithms. Numerical simulation results indicated that the interpolation-tube-based constrained iterative linear quadratic regulator (itube-CILQR) algorithm outperformed solvers based on the standard Interior Point Optimizer (IPOPT) and Operator Splitting Quadratic Program (OSQP), achieving a mean computation time of less than 5 ms. The itube-CILQR algorithm was then used for vehicle control in vision-based lane-keeping experiments in a simulation environment to quantify the perceptual uncertainty of a visual perception model (multitask UNet). This quantification was performed using the OF-MPC framework for traffic scenes that were not encountered during model training. Overall, the developed OF-MPC framework and the proposed method for characterizing the perceptual uncertainty of a visual perception system in unseen environments can contribute to the future development of advanced autonomous driving systems.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are typically pretrained on broad, diverse datasets and subsequently fine-tuned on a comparatively small dataset tailored to a specific target robot. Such adaptation recipe is now widely established, achieving its most mature results on fixed-base arms due to their static base dynamics and consistent reference frame. Mounting the same arm on a quadruped extends the workspace to the entire floor, but fundamentally changes the embodiment: rather than controlling the arm in isolation, the policy must jointly coordinate base navigation, body posture, and end-effector motion. In this work, we study the practical demands of this embodiment change across data collection, model adaptation, and inference design to achieve closed-loop loco-manipulation on a Unitree Go2 with an AgileX Piper arm. We report the choices that made this real-robot deployment feasible, namely model variant, action horizon, and demonstration conventions, together with a study tailored to a pick-and-place task, separating data coverage from model capacity. We show that expanding demonstration coverage reduces held-out end-effector error by 30%, and incorporating secondary skills (such as pushing rather than grasping) introduces new behavioral modes without degrading baseline performance. Finally, closed-loop real-world experiments show how dataset composition shapes success and failure, validating the pipeline for more complex loco-manipulation.

</details>


### [286] [Delay Randomization Widens the Delay Margin of Learned Humanoid Motion Trackers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32277)
*Shaoqin Li,Yi Sun*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Learned humanoid controllers are usually trained in one simulator and then checked in a second one before they go near hardware. We ask what that check says about command delay. We train motion-tracking policies for a 24-joint humanoid on five motions taken from monocular video and test them in an independent CPU MuJoCo harness, which we first verify against the training model. Every policy completes its motion in at least 26 of 30 episodes under the training randomization. With 40 ms of constant command delay, however, survival drops to 15% on average (0% - 45%), and the delay margin is only 20 - 30 ms. Randomizing the delay during training (0 - 50 ms) lifts survival at 40 ms to 95% on average (75% - 100%) and moves the margin to 50 - 70 ms. A control arm trained for the same number of extra iterations without delay does not improve, so the gain comes from the randomization itself. This holds for all five motions with three training seeds each, and a policy trained in PhysX behaves the same way. The price is slightly looser tracking (0.02 - 0.06 rad on four motions) and, for the most dynamic motion, less robustness to other perturbations. We also show that the first sim-to-sim gap we saw came from the harness, a missing floor and a wrong timestep, and not from the physics engines. The 0 - 20 ms delay ranges common in locomotion training lie inside the margin an unrandomized tracker already has. The benefit comes from randomizing beyond it, so the range should be set from the robot's measured latency, and delay margin belongs next to other sim-to-sim metrics.

</details>

Abstract: Generalist robot manipulation requires a single vision-language-action (VLA) policy to execute diverse skills across scenes and instructions. However, current multi-skill VLA policies often leave two aspects of an instruction insufficiently modeled: which primitive skill to execute and where/how to contact and move objects. We propose MAST-VLA (Multi-skill Adaptation with Skill-conditioned experts and Trajectory-affordance priors), a dual-conditioning framework for robot manipulation. MAST-VLA introduces skill-conditioned LoRA Mixture-of-Experts (LoRA-MoE) adapters in the LLM backbone, where skill-query tokens and routing biases guide low-rank expert specialization, and converts a predicted affordance heatmap into a patch-level trajectory-affordance spatial prior for the action head. On the CALVIN ABC$\rightarrow$D long-horizon multi-skill cross-scene generalization setting, MAST-VLA achieves state-of-the-art performance, reaching an Avg. Len of 4.65 over five-subtask evaluation sequences. Real-robot multi-skill sequence experiments further validate its deployability under real perception and control noise.

</details>


### [288] [DynaSlots: Slot-Centric 3D Dynamics Pretraining for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32330)

Abstract: Robot manipulation involves localized changes: picking up a cup moves the cup while leaving the surrounding scene largely unchanged. Yet existing 3D visual pretraining methods, including recent dynamics-aware variants, model state transitions at the scene level, compressing the motion of everything in a scene into a single global latent action. This conflates semantically distinct transitions in scenes with multiple moving parts, obscures dependencies between changing regions, and leaves the learned representation vulnerable to task-irrelevant scene content. We introduce DynaSlots, an action-free, label-free pretraining framework that decomposes each point cloud frame into a small set of temporally consistent slots and models dynamics at this per-slot level rather than the scene level. A temporal slot propagation module promotes consistent slot assignments across frames; an inverse dynamics model then extracts one latent action per slot from feature differences, and a diffusion-based forward dynamics model, paired with a self-supervised interaction head, predicts each slot's future state while learning pairwise proximity-and-motion relations. Experiments on Meta-World, Adroit, and a RealMan RM65-B robot demonstrate that DynaSlots outperforms prior scene-level baselines. It achieves superior success rates in simulation and exhibits strong robustness to scene clutter and unseen objects in real-world manipulation. Code is available at \href{https://zhucezhanghao12345-source.github.io/DynaSlots-Anonymous/}{https://zhucezhanghao12345-source.github.io/DynaSlots-Anonymous/}.

</details>

Abstract: Many manipulation tasks require a robot to remember what it has done and what it has seen. Frontier vision-language models can plan such tasks without task-specific training, but how such a planner should remember an episode is an open question. We argue that its memory must keep track of what the planner has done and learned, and must let the planner choose what to look at, since every frame costs tokens; a note rewritten at every step meets both poorly. We propose LogLook, whose execution-grounded memory lets the system keep the record, an execution log and the model's earlier notes, while the model decides where to look: frames are returned only where it expects a change, and demonstrations are skimmed before it inspects the parts it chooses. Measured with the benchmark's motion planner, LogLook matches the success of a note-only memory with 43% fewer tokens and less than half the images, and fails far fewer episodes through what it remembered or saw. With direct control, LogLook solves 93.5% of the RoboMME test set.

</details>

Abstract: We present the Graphs of Trajectory Bundles (GTB) method for motion planning of systems with nonlinear and black-box dynamics, combining sampling-based motion planning with trajectory optimization and graph search. Based on a graph-of-convex-sets decomposition of the workspace, GTB iteratively constructs a graph of trajectory bundles that captures the local dynamics of the system at any vertex. By solving a shortest-path problem on this graph, we find maximally dynamically feasible iterates that satisfy the system's dynamics in the limit and any constraints such as collision avoidance or additional safety or specification constraints by construction of the underlying graph. As a result, GTB can handle highly nonlinear, unstable, and even black-box dynamics with complex collision and specification constraints, making it a powerful tool for motion planning of complex systems.

</details>

Abstract: Designing effective reward functions is a critical challenge in Reinforcement Learning (RL), which traditionally requires costly manual trial-and-error. While Large Language Model (LLM)-based methods have shown promise in reward design, undifferentiated sampling and limited use of historical feedback often lead to insufficient exploration of the reward space and optimization instability. To address these challenges, we propose $R^2E$, a Role-driven Reward Evolutionary framework for automated reward function design that structures the process through explicit role specialization. $R^2E$ employs multiple LLM roles with complementary objectives: an Explorer that promotes novelty to expand the search space, a Guardian that performs conservative refinements to improve training stability, and an Evolver that recombines high-performing rewards via crossover and mutation to integrate effective structures. A dynamic scheduling strategy coordinates these roles, progressively shifting the search from broad exploration to focused exploitation. To mitigate optimization instability and provide a medium for cross-role collaboration, $R^2E$ incorporates a Global Elite Pool that retains the best-performing rewards to guide subsequent generations, thereby enabling coordinated interaction among roles and ensuring a reliable and consistent refinement process. Extensive experiments on multiple robotic tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our method.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in large-scale human motion data, motion retargeting, and motion imitation have enabled increasingly capable whole-body control for individual humanoids. Cooperative carrying is more challenging because successful coordination requires not only plausible individual motions, but also consistent cross-agent and agent-object relations, including shared grasp, relative configuration, and whole-body support. We introduce CoCarry, a cooperative-carrying dataset containing 220 synchronized two-person sequences across 21 task schemes, five shared-object geometries, four terrain types, and approximately 61 minutes of motion. We further propose Contact-Support-Structured Retargeting, which starts from nominal individual motions and jointly refines both humanoids around the same prescribed object trajectory. Cooperation-aware object-local hand anchors preserve compatible four-hand grasp relations, while complementary upper object-interaction and lower support structures retain object-relative geometry and coordinated support. Across all 220 CoCarry sequences retargeted to two A3 humanoids, our method achieves 0.9995 contact preservation, reduces penetration duration by 67.0% and 71.0% relative to GMR and OmniRetarget, respectively, and maintains a 100% retargeting success rate. On CORE4D with Unitree G1 humanoids, it achieves 0.9906 contact preservation with zero measured sliding. We further validate the generated references through physics simulation and multiple real-world cooperative carrying experiments on two physical A3 humanoids, demonstrating their usability for downstream dual-humanoid control.

</details>

Abstract: Collective robotics is split by a long-standing but increasingly artificial boundary between centralised multi-robot systems that prioritise optimality and decentralised swarms that prioritise robustness and adaptivity. We propose a unified control framework, shifting from `obligate' swarms---systems constrained to pure self-organisation---to facultatively swarming systems: hybrid architectures that operate as centrally planned systems under permissive conditions yet degrade gracefully into decentralised swarming under environmental stress. By reframing swarming as a dynamic capability, rather than a rigid system category, we outline a pathway towards collective robotic systems that benefit from centralised optimality when conditions permit and from the flexibility of self-organisation when they do not.

</details>

Abstract: Ultrasound imaging is highly operator-dependent, motivating robotic systems that reproduce expert scanning skills consistently. These skills involve coordinated interpretation of anatomical landmarks, ultrasound images, and probe-tissue interactions that are difficult to encode in hand-designed control strategies. Imitation learning offers an alternative, but its observation and action representations should reflect the anatomically structured nature of scanning. We propose an anatomy-anchored imitation-learning framework that combines anatomy-guided probe placement with learned ultrasound navigation. The placement module identifies a task-specific anatomical starting point from external visual observations and establishes the initial probe pose as the reference frame for scanning. A flow-based policy then generates scanning motions from anchor-relative probe poses, ultrasound images, probe-mounted camera views, and contact forces, while a termination head predicts task completion. This avoids continuous reliance on third-person vision and reduces dependence on the robot-patient configuration. A handheld acquisition platform enables multimodal demonstrations to be collected during natural freehand scanning without a robot. On 16 unseen participants, task success reaches 96%, 94%, 88%, and 94% for kidney, gallbladder, and liver standard-plane acquisition and radial artery sweeping, respectively. Compared with a fine-tuned pi_0 model, success on the abdominal tasks improves by 10-42 percentage points, with lower time-to-target, peak contact force, and trajectory jerk. Ablations support the complementary roles of anatomical anchoring, egocentric vision, and force feedback. End-to-end trials with automatic placement achieve 90-100% success, and the policies transfer without retraining to a second robot with 83-100% success.

</details>

Abstract: Supervising a multi-UAV reconnaissance mission usually means plotting waypoints and watching every drone. We present UAV SwarmAgent, a hierarchical multi-agent system in which an operator states the mission in one natural-language sentence and a ground-station agent writes and executes code that turns it into per-UAV routes. The agent grounds inspection targets in an aerial map with a vision-language model, splits them among N UAVs by balancing route length, and dispatches worker agents that verify incidents with a compact onboard decision model. In simulation over 4 incident types and 3 foundation models, the swarm located the incident in 244 of 255 missions (95.7%). A paired scaling study with one to 5 UAVs shows that flight time follows an Amdahl-type law. The second UAV delivers half of the attainable saving and the speedup is bounded at 1.52x. We discuss what this predictability, and the false alarms we observed, imply for an operator who supervises the swarm, and outline the user study needed to test it.

</details>

Abstract: Zero-shot object navigation requires an embodied agent to choose where to search while retaining evidence from previous observations. HGWM couples an LLM-generated room-anchor prior with persistent spatial-semantic memory. A coarse stage assigns visual-semantic scores to candidate headings on a 0-10 scale; a fine stage measures symbolic compatibility between observed objects, room context, and the goal prior. Phase-dependent fusion combines these scores with an LLM direction recommendation before local action selection. On HM3D v0.1, the reported HGWM configuration achieves 62.6% success rate (SR) and 31.5% success weighted by path length (SPL), compared with 58.1% and 31.2% for WMNav. On MP3D, the corresponding values are 46.8% and 17.9%, compared with 45.4% and 17.2%. The results indicate higher success point estimates and modest path-efficiency gains. On HM3D v0.2, the full model obtains 74.4% SR and 31.9% SPL. Additional component ablations reduce SR by 0.9-2.3 percentage points and SPL by 0.6-1.5 points. These are author-reported aggregate measurements. The accompanying implementation repairs pass offline tests; episode-level rerun records are not available. Evaluation is confined to simulation.

Abstract: Semantics can provide a proxy for physical traversability. Terrain that looks like loose sand or a rock field is likely to have similar physical properties. Open-vocabulary vision-language models (VLMs) enable this physical reasoning over terrain outside any fixed label set. We present Open-world Language-conditioned Speed Fields (OWLS), which plans directly over the dense feature map of a VLM. Prompted with a description of traversable terrain, the VLM scores every pixel of a single camera image; OWLS projects these scores onto the ground, adapts them to the platform with two morphological operations, and uses the result as the speed field of a minimum-time planner, so terrain appearance and obstacle clearance shape one trajectory. OWLS needs no task-specific training, no terrain annotation, no persistent map, and no exteroceptive sensor beyond the camera. We evaluate it on a physical rover indoors and off-road, on simulated Mars and Moon rovers over 200 procedurally generated terrains, and on real planetary imagery.

</details>

Abstract: Indoor UAV localization with minimal onboard sensing remains challenging, particularly in dynamic environments. We propose augmented filtered Monte Carlo localization (AF-MCL) for 2D localization using only four single-point range sensors and measured heading. AF-MCL rejects range measurements inconsistent with the known static map and adaptively injects particles to recover from localization divergence and kidnapping. Beam classification is performed only at the previous position estimate, making its ray-casting cost independent of the particle count. The particle distribution additionally provides an online measure of localization uncertainty. Real-world experiments in a 2.10x1.63 m environment yield mean position errors of 6.4-7.7 cm under static and dynamic conditions. Simulations in 6x6 m and 10x10 m environments yield 4.5-5.2 cm mean errors; under dynamic obstacles, standard MCL reaches 12.3 cm and reliable MCL 7.7 cm. The results show that robust indoor localization under dynamic obstacles is achievable with only four single-point range measurements and heading.

Abstract: Autonomous mobile robots support material transport in warehouses and industrial facilities, where navigation must adapt to changing operating conditions. Sensor observations reveal physical obstacles, but do not by themselves specify temporary access rules, route preferences, or exceptions known to operators. This paper presents a natural-language policy interface that brings this knowledge into ROS 2 Nav2. A local large language model converts instructions into JSON policies specifying destinations, spatial constraints, and temporal or event conditions. Deterministic checks govern mission acceptance, and three costmap adapters project accepted policies into the existing navigation stack without changing its planner or controller. On 150 researcher-authored English commands, four local models produce 107-142 correct accepted policies, compared with 44 for a rule-based parser, with 3-20 incorrect acceptances per model. Fault injection records rejection of eight endpoint/JSON faults and 29 validator-stage faults. Navigation experiments use correct task policies to evaluate keepouts, zone costs, person-dependent regions, and event-conditioned passage in simulation and on a TurtleBot3. For Tasks 1-3, mean travel times are 18.4-24.5% lower in simulation and 23.1-35.0% lower on the physical testbed than with the baseline lacking those policies. A native KeepoutFilter comparison and two-robot demonstrations examine basic keepout behavior and shared policy projection. The interface connects operator instructions to explicit navigation policies while retaining Nav2's planning and control components.

</details>

Abstract: Unrecognized anomalies in critical infrastructure can rapidly cascade into catastrophic physical or operational failures. Current anomaly detection approaches often rely on direct access to the monitored system by utilizing onboard sensors, such as motor encoders, to detect anomalous states. However, legacy hardware often lacks these sensors, or strict security protocols may prohibit access altogether. We therefore present AWARE, a world model framework that is capable of anomaly detection using only external camera monitoring via system dynamics estimation and state prediction. Evaluated across 240 real-world robotic crane trajectories, including payload collisions and motor degradations, AWARE demonstrates a 26% improvement in anomaly classification over our strongest baseline under noisy, camera-only state estimation. Furthermore, the inferred dynamics parameter can be decoded to identify the source of faults, localizing the degrading motor with 88% accuracy.

</details>


### [303] [Semantic Representation for Agentic Self-Play in Automated Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32755)
*Yves Delley,John Coller,Zachary Cutts,Alexander Daryin,Mathias Bellaiche,Jun W. Kim,Oscar Donosa,Srinidhi B. Seshadri,Tanner Clary,Sandy Klemm,Avinatan Hassidim,Yossi Matias,Pooja Rao,Petar Sirkovic,Annalisa Pawlosky*

Abstract: Navigating complex, high-dimensional biological search spaces necessitates scalable physical and computational intelligence to explore beyond local optima through iterative self-play across ideation, experimentation, and automation. Translating experimental designs into reliable physical execution remains bottlenecked by static laboratory automation and unmodeled physical variability. To address this, we introduce VERified Infrastructure for Discovery Laboratories, or VERID, a foundational infrastructure designed to bridge the gap between the high-level reasoning of artificial intelligence (AI) agents and complex, error-prone physical realities of wet labs. VERID is a persistent, high-fidelity semantic digital twin of the laboratory workcell that provides four core services: queryable introspection for agentic reasoning, time-compressed simulation for protocol validation, comprehensive trace persistence for a complete scientific record, and a framework for safe execution.
  We present three case studies using aqueous dye and deionized water mixtures to validate the optical and fluidic foundations of VERID. These studies demonstrate how VERID enables pre-emptive detection of critical errors in experimental protocols, stateful mid-run error recovery and post-hoc identification of subtle physical failures. The simulation functions as a discrete accounting engine, allowing agents to safely and inexpensively explore experimental designs in silico and refine experimental strategies through iterative self-play. VERID also captures structured multimodal experimental data for downstream analysis, knowledge mining and agent training. We argue that this semantic layer is an essential prerequisite for deploying autonomous agents in the biochemical and biomedical sciences, ensuring data integrity, operational safety, and paving the way for tackling previously intractable grand challenges in biology.

</details>

Abstract: Quantitative and dense task progress feedback is essential for robotic manipulation across data curation, policy evaluation, and reinforcement learning. Existing progress annotations often rely on sparse keyframe markings derived from subjective manual judgments, limiting their scalability and cross-task consistency, particularly in long-horizon manipulation. To overcome these challenges, we propose action-aware quantification, a unified progress specification that decomposes long-horizon trajectories into object-level subtasks via a vision-language model (VLM) and assigns weighted action credits to capture fine-grained execution dynamics. Using VLM auto-labeling and human calibration, we construct an 800+ hour training video dataset and LOOM, a long-horizon progress benchmark containing 19,618 human-calibrated evaluation frames. To model these dense progress dynamics, we introduce NimCritic, a predictive progress critic trained via Joint Video-Progress Learning. We further incorporate Historical Progress Perturbation to mitigate sequential drift, and design a contrastive Video-Progress Alignment objective that bridges asymmetric expert streams by mutually grounding intermediate visual dynamics and denoised progress trajectories. NimCritic matches or outperforms state-of-the-art process reward models (PRMs) on the established Video Progress Benchmark (VPB) and LOOM, surpassing GVL (GPT-6-Sol) on VPB by about 65% in terminal success accuracy (TSA). On the large-scale RoboArena policy evaluation benchmark, it also retrieves human-selected successful rollouts with about 97% overall recall. These results establish action-aware quantification as a scalable foundation for dense progress feedback in robotic manipulation.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies can adapt to local human intervention, while federated learning can combine distributed robot experience without pooling raw records. We examine their prospective intersection: sharing adaptations derived from diverse human corrections across tasks, environments, people, and robot embodiments. Corrections differ in modality, safety semantics(urgency, behavioral scope, etc.), and privacy sensitivity. A shared update that improves mean task success may suppress a rare safety intervention or produce an unsafe response on another robot. We argue for three requirements: preserve the context and uncertainty of multimodal corrections when deciding what to share; assess candidate updates in each receiving robot's closed loop before deployment; and evaluate leakage alongside retention of safety-critical evidence. Immediate, legible defensive behavior remains necessary during human interaction. We offer counterexamples and a falsifiable evaluation agenda, not experimental results.

</details>

Abstract: HoloBoxing presents a simple recipe for learning humanoid boxing by building on a pretrained whole-body controller. The recipe separates reusable motor skills from task decisions: a compact planner requests future body-point targets, while the frozen controller handles joint-level execution. A learned decoder maps 32-dimensional actions to structured target windows, and measured-state imitation initializes the planner before reinforcement learning. Task learning then refines interaction behavior while reusing the decoder and controller, avoiding the need to relearn whole-body control. This combination keeps task optimization focused on a compact upper policy and supplies an initial behavior before reward-driven exploration. Starting from that available controller, the recipe accounts for about seven minutes of additional training, including reused representation fitting and a single-GPU task run. This excludes controller pretraining, data preparation, evaluation, and search. Simulated boxing experiments and component controls evaluate the resulting behavior and the roles of imitation initialization and the target representation.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models perform well on robotic manipulation, yet remain brittle once their own actions carry them into states their training data barely covers. Recovering from such failures usually requires recovery demonstrations, policy retraining, or a separate model at inference time. We introduce Backtrack, a lightweight failure detection and recovery mechanism for frozen VLAs that needs none of these. A small failure prediction head, trained only on outcome-labeled rollouts of the frozen policy, reads the VLA's existing internal representations to estimate whether the current episode will fail. When the predicted failure probability exceeds a threshold, a controller pauses the policy, retraces the arm's recently achieved motion, and hands control back to the unchanged policy so it can retry from an earlier configuration. The head adds only 0.48-0.88% to the policy's parameter count. We evaluate Backtrack on LIBERO with three VLAs spanning two action-generation families: SmolVLA and $π$0.5, which use flow-matching action experts, and the autoregressive $π$0-FAST. On LIBERO-Long, the suite of long-horizon multi-step tasks and the one on which all three policies fail most often, Backtrack reduces each policy's failure rate by 19-48%, raising success by up to 7.4 percentage points; on the three shorter suites, where control success is already 85-99%, its effect is smaller. On MetaWorld, all twelve controller configurations tested with SmolVLA improve on the unassisted policy.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce RynnUnify, a 16B unified embodied model that combines strong embodied understanding with long-horizon visual planning through interleaved video--language reasoning. With capabilities spanning spatial reasoning, physical prediction, and grounding, RynnUnify connects an understanding of the physical world with visual anticipation of how it evolves across successive subtasks. Building on these capabilities, we further develop an agentic extension through reinforcement learning to selectively use video predictions for reasoning about unobserved states and interaction outcomes. We also develop a world-action extension of the full RynnUnify model, translating its understanding and prediction capabilities into robot control. To evaluate these capabilities, we introduce Rynn-VP and Rynn-GEU for long-horizon visual planning and anticipatory reasoning. Experiments show that RynnUnify achieves the highest average embodied understanding score among the evaluated unified multimodal models and improves the Rynn-VP overall score by 9.75 points over Cosmos 3 Nano. Its agentic extension improves the Rynn-GEU average by 6.0 points over direct reasoning, while the world-action extension achieves 51.5\% success on 20 selected composite RoboCasa365 tasks and 46.7\% average success across three real-robot tasks. Together, these results establish RynnUnify as a shared foundation for embodied understanding, visual planning, and robot control, taking a step toward a unified embodied brain.

</details>


### [309] [Robotic Optical Coherence Tomography-Guided Geometric Modeling of Soft Tissue: 3D Representations for Data-Driven Closed-Loop Shape Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32961)

Abstract: In medical robotics, data-driven soft-tissue shape control requires deforming tissue toward desired morphologies through sequential physical interactions. This challenge is particularly important in microscale manipulation, where localized deformation is difficult to observe, represent, and control, while physics-based models can be computationally expensive and difficult to adapt to realistic environments. Optical coherence tomography (OCT) provides high-resolution volumetric perception of tissue deformation, but target-conditioned control requires effective representations of both observed and desired tissue states. In this work, we introduce an OCT-guided data-driven framework for tissue shape control, learned from user-guided demonstrations and executed with closed-loop OCT feedback. We study full-volume, tissue-masked sparse-volume, and voxelized point-cloud representations, with or without residual conditioning, using deterministic (Explicit) and generative diffusion (Diffusion) policies. Across 12 configurations and 576 phantom rollouts on 3-DoF depth and 6-DoF directional tasks, OCT-derived 3D representations support both Explicit and Diffusion policies, with performance depending on representation and residual conditioning. Residual conditioning reduces directional Shape RMSE from 0.748 to 0.555 mm for full-volume Diffusion, while voxelized point-cloud Explicit with residuals achieves 0.534 mm for depth and 0.443 mm for directional control, with comparable accuracy at intermediate unseen targets and consistent performance across deployments. We further evaluate the framework in 60 ex vivo porcine rollouts, extending to biological tissue with final geometric errors comparable to human demonstrations. These results establish volumetric OCT as current and goal representations for learned closed-loop tissue shape control across deterministic and generative action models.

</details>

Abstract: Learning humanoid control from monocular human videos offers a scalable way to acquire diverse whole-body behaviors. Yet, existing reconstruction-retargeting-tracking pipelines are limited in system-level scalability by separately optimized intermediate stages and cannot directly optimize reference motion executability using downstream tracking feedback. We present ViT-H, a video-to-humanoid control framework following a generation-and-tracking paradigm. ViT-H directly generates humanoid reference motions from videos with a diffusion model, bypassing human motion reconstruction and retargeting, and uses motion-tracking outcomes to post-train the generator with direct preference optimization. We further introduce an autoregressive variant that enables real-time humanoid video teleoperation with sub-400 ms latency. Extensive experiments on the Unitree G1 demonstrate that ViT-H outperforms state-of-the-art reconstruction-retargeting-tracking pipelines in both motion quality and executability. We further show that ViT-H scales favorably with model size and with both pre-training and post-training data scale, while motion-tracking feedback consistently improves executability across different motion trackers.

</details>

Abstract: Collecting high-quality demonstrations remains a central bottleneck for learning dexterous manipulation. Teleoperation records executable robot actions, but it requires a complete robot setup and places the burden of coordinating many finger joints on the operator. Human demonstrations are easier to collect, but their motions must be estimated and retargeted to the robot, and they do not directly provide robot actions. In this paper, we introduce DexPort, a portable data-collection interface for high-DoF robot hands. It uses the same hand hardware for collection and deployment, avoiding a hand embodiment gap, and records both commanded joint targets and measured joint states. Our key observation is that a small set of skills can support a wide range of dexterous tasks. During collection, the operator moves the hand-held interface to specify the robot wrist motion and selects or adjusts skills that coordinate the finger motion. This removes the need to control each joint independently while retaining behaviors such as precise grasping and in-hand manipulation. We collect demonstrations across several real-world tasks and in unstructured environments without a robot arm. We then use behavior cloning to train visuomotor policies directly on the recorded wrist motions and hand commands, without skill labels. We evaluate across 4 tasks: pick-and-place with diverse objects, screwing with in-hand tool use, drawer stowage which requires simultaneous grasp and pulling, and a bimanual scanning task. We collect over 2,500 demonstrations with our portable device, in both laboratory and in-the-wild settings. Across our tasks, we see an improvement from 11% to 64% when using recorded robot commands instead of states, and an improvement from 36% to 78% for task success when using in-the-wild data.

</details>

Abstract: Reliability investments compete for constrained capital but are often evaluated in separate programs. This paper presents a data-driven framework that translates historical work orders, geographic asset records, outage events, and costs into comparable customer-minute and customer-interruption benefits at the circuit-zone level. A normalization process maps historical device names and non-isolating assets to current automaticisolating-device zones and links investments to pre- and postconstruction outage histories. Utility data covering more than $250 million in investments completed from 2010 to 2020 are used to estimate benefit factors for seven work types. Those factors feed a mixed-integer portfolio model that selects at most one applicable action per zone under a capital budget. Observed reductions range from 32% to 94% for customer-minutes interrupted and from 43% to 93% for customer interruptions. The framework provides a traceable path from heterogeneous utility records to zone-level reliability investment decisions

### [313] [Exposing Noise-Deficiency Signatures of Deep Learning-Based False Data Injection Attacks Through AC State Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31799)
*Yichen Liu,Hongyu Wu,Bo Liu*

Abstract: Deep learning (DL)-based false data injection (FDI) attacks can resemble normal measurements and evade both conventional bad data detection (BDD) and measurement-space data-driven detectors. This paper identifies a previously overlooked signature of such attacks in state-estimation (SE) residual space. The residuals of reconstruction-based DL attacks exhibit deficient stochastic variation, manifested as abnormally small residual energy and reduced directional dispersion. We establish the conditions under which this signature emerges and explain why the attacks carrying it can evade conventional BDD. We show that SE can reveal residual-space differences obscured by operating-state variation in measurement space. We then explain the origin of the signature through the noise-suppression mechanism of DL attack generation. A generator can learn the reproducible structure of historical measurements but cannot reproduce stochastic measurement components that are unpredictable from its input. This signature enables label-free detection using a simple two-sided test on the conventional BDD statistic, while the full residual vector can also be exploited when labeled attack samples are available. Experiments on the IEEE 14-bus and 118-bus AC systems show that the label-free test detects up to 99% of three representative DL attacks at a false-alarm rate comparable to that of BDD, which detects at most 5%. Ablation studies further examine the effects of residual representation, decision rule, and measurement-noise magnitude and distribution.

</details>

Abstract: This paper considers the identifiability of dynamical networks whose topology is a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG), in the context where not all nodes are excited and not all nodes are measured. The objective is to construct a valid and parsimonious
  Excitation and Measurement Pattern (EMP), i.e. a selection of excited and measured nodes, that allows to identify the whole network with a small number of excitations and measurements. We show first that a DAG can be decomposed into a set of trees and parallel paths networks. We propose an algorithm that achieves this decomposition and computes an EMP that combines EMPs for the individual trees and parallel paths networks.
  We formally show that this algorithm yields an EMP capable of identifying the whole network.

</details>

Abstract: Existing stealthy attacks presuppose white- or grey-box access to the target system, unrealistic in practice. This paper develops a model-free stealthy attack framework that learns an attack policy directly from nominal measurement data, without knowledge of the system dynamics, estimator, or detector. We establish a certified residual-impact bound and an observable stealth budget around a stealth-consistent anchor, and show that recurrence and context conditioning are structural necessities for closed-loop, multivariate cyber-physical systems (CPS). The resulting attack generator, trained against a Wasserstein critic under a stealth-constrained impact objective, induces a hazardous 2.51 m lane departure while evading five intrusion detection systems, and, in real-time hardware-in-the-loop tests, a speed deviation of approximately 8 km/h while staying below the detection threshold. Results show that safety-critical CPS remain exposed without attacker access to privileged information, motivating offense-informed defenses.

</details>

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of frequency-domain system identification with bounded measurement noise. We focus on interpolatory identification algorithms, which return models consistent with both the data and a priori constraints. For equally spaced samples, we sharpen previous worst-case bounds on the consistency-set radius. Its excess above the noise level decays as $N^{-2}$, where $N$ is the number of samples, and a matching lower bound shows that this rate is tight. We then examine whether bounded stochastic noise allows identification below the noise level. We show that high-probability error bounds can converge to zero. Their convergence rate depends on how strongly the noise distribution is concentrated near the boundary of the noise set. For example, noise uniformly distributed on a disk gives an upper bound of order $(\log N/N)^{2/3}$. Numerical experiments examine these convergence rates and the effect of the noise distribution on the identification error.

</details>

Abstract: This paper addresses the problem of practical tracking control for robotic vehicles with gimbaled unidirectional actuators, with special application to multirotor unmanned aerial vehicles. A widely recognized obstacle to tracking control for multirotors is the free-fall singularity --- when a multirotor is commanded to free fall, the vehicle's target attitude becomes undefined. This singularity is a pathology pertinent to all robotic vehicles with gimbaled unidirectional actuators. This paper considers the general theoretical problem for vehicles with gimbaled actuators. Starting from a baseline robust controller that assumes unconstrained inputs, we redesign the control law to be compatible with the gimbaled actuator. This is accomplished by driving the output of the gimbaled actuator to a ball centered at the target input value. The baseline controller's tracking guarantees are recovered in a practical sense. We apply our results to synthesize a global practical complete position tracking controller for multirotor unmanned aerial vehicles. The theory is demonstrated in simulation.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we present a novel method for the localization and temporal waveform reconstruction of disturbances in complex power grids using observations of system state variables. Although various methods exist for detecting these events, accurately identifying and responding to them remains a significant challenge due to the grid's size, speed, complexity, and inherently dynamic behavior. We formulate a method for disturbance localization and reconstruction based on reservoir computing, a recurrent neural network architecture. We show that our method is able to recover the time dependence of previously unobserved disturbances, is robust to missing information, and can operate online. The approach requires only observed system data and knowledge of the network topology, without requiring a physical model of the system dynamics, and can recover simultaneous disturbances occurring at multiple locations. We implement a scalable parallelized reservoir architecture and demonstrate our algorithm on test IEEE cases and a reduced WECC system.

</details>

Abstract: Cascaded DC systems may become unstable through source-load impedance interactions even when their constituent converters are locally stable. This paper proposes a source-side model-free control (MFC) method that reuses bus-voltage feedback to reshape the source output impedance while preserving nominal reference-to-output dynamics. The controller uses only nominal source-loop information and requires neither a load-converter model nor load-side measurements. A current-controlled buck source supplying a regulated buck-resistor load that exhibits constant-power load (CPL) behaviour at low frequencies is used as the case study. Impedance analysis predicts that the proposed MFC increases the maximum stable load power by approximately 50%, thereby improving the stability margin of the cascaded system. Switched-model simulations confirm preservation of the nominal reference-to-output response and show that the MFC-controlled system remains stable at load levels for which the baseline system becomes unstable.

</details>

Abstract: Grid-connected inverters can be unstable when their current-control loops interact with an uncertain external network. Returning the existing controller to improve damping also alters current tracking because tracking dynamics and output admittance are coupled. This paper adds a local model-free control (MFC) loop around the existing current controller to reduce output admittance while closely preserving nominal current-tracking dynamics. The added loop uses local inverter parameters without an external grid or neighbouring converter model. A Norton formulation separates reference tracking from the current response to terminal-voltage disturbances and establishes a tracking-preservation condition for admittance reshaping. Reducing the disturbance response makes the inverter behave more like an ideal controlled current source. Averaged-model simulations show reductions exceeding 65% in the magnitudes of all four dq output-admittance elements over 10-200 Hz, with closely preserved current-tracking dynamics. Generalised Nyquist analysis predicts small-signal stability for all sampled grid-impedance cases with MFC, compared with 90.0% without MFC.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents a guidance method that enables a quadrotor uncrewed aerial vehicle to perform inspection tasks along an elliptical boundary enclosing a structure of interest. The proposed guidance approach leverages a bifurcation theory-based framework that supports multiple inspection tasks, including convergence to a desired viewpoint, switching between viewpoints along the boundary, constant-speed elliptical circumnavigation, and circumnavigation with sectorial dwell-time constraint. Stability analysis of the proposed guidance dynamics establishes convergence of the generated reference trajectories to the desired behavior. The control parameters in the proposed method further allow the settling time of the reference trajectory to be specified for each task. The resulting reference velocity expressions are closed-form and algebraically simple, facilitating practical implementation. A qualitative comparison with existing guidance methods highlights the flexibility of the proposed approach in achieving different inspection tasks through a unified framework, while relying on simpler guidance computations. The proposed guidance method is validated through numerical simulations using a six-degree-of-freedom quadrotor model with model uncertainties, and a cascaded sliding-mode controller that tracks the generated reference trajectories under bounded disturbances.

</details>

Abstract: Lower space-launch cost and advances in AI are driving space-based data centers (SDCs) that provide AI-centric computing and communication services to in-orbit and non-terrestrial assets. However, architectural principles, service models, and energy provisioning for such SDCs, particularly in low-Earth orbit (LEO), remain largely unexplored. We introduce heterogeneous SDCs composed of large and small nodes, differentiated by computing capability, size, and power budget, to support applications spanning ground, aerial, and in-orbit users. We define use cases, node sizing and placement strategies, and interconnection topologies integrating space-to-space and space-to-ground links. Focusing on dawn-dusk LEO configurations, we analyze solar power availability, visibility windows, and latency-aware coverage. Using baseline workloads for typical scenarios, we evaluate latency and service reliability. We quantify energy requirements of different SDC roles and deployment scales, highlighting trade-offs among node size, orbit selection, and service portfolio, and provide design guidance and future research directions for AI infrastructure in space.

</details>

Abstract: Max-pressure (MP) control adapts signal phases to prevailing traffic states, but its decision timescale is typically fixed a priori. This paper treats the decision timescale as an explicit dimension of MP control. We first characterize the trade-off between responsiveness and switching efficiency by quantifying how an equivalent switching loss reduces service capacity over a finite decision interval. This yields a load-dependent critical timescale determined by switching loss and normalized traffic load, together with a performance-oriented operating range above the stability boundary. Building on this result, we propose Variable-Timescale Max-Pressure (VT-MP), in which each intersection selects its decision interval according to local operating conditions, allowing the control timescale to vary across time and space. We establish strong stability of the resulting asynchronous heterogeneous controller under uniformly admissible local loads and bounded decision intervals, and introduce a downstream receiving-supply correction for finite-storage implementation. Three experiments provide mechanism validation and network-level evaluation. A single-intersection experiment confirms that the preferred decision interval increases systematically with traffic load and follows the derived characteristic timescales. In a store-and-forward network, heterogeneous VT-MP reduces total queueing time by 31.0% relative to the best fixed-interval benchmark. In a microscopic 5x5 SUMO network, it reduces mean trip delay by 24.3%, from 213.3 s to 161.5 s, and peak network accumulation by 12.3%. These findings show that the MP decision timescale should be treated as a traffic-state-dependent control variable rather than a fixed implementation parameter.

</details>


### [324] [Regret-Based Max-Pressure Traffic Signal Control under Uncertain Traffic States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32290)

Abstract: Max-pressure (MP) traffic signal control selects phases using pressure scores computed from estimated traffic states, but these states are uncertain and their consequences depend on whether they alter phase ranking and how much pressure is lost. This paper develops a decision-oriented Regret-Based Max-Pressure framework that represents uncertainty through phase-selection regret, defined as the pressure loss of a candidate phase relative to the pressure-maximizing phase under each plausible latent state. We propose Stochastic Regret MP, which minimizes expected regret under a calibrated residual distribution, and Distributionally Robust (DR) Regret MP, which minimizes worst-case expected regret over a Wasserstein ambiguity set. Robustifying regret rather than absolute pressure makes the formulation invariant to state-dependent pressure components shared by all candidate phases, thereby focusing robustness on relative phase quality. We further derive an explicit queue-linear pressure-loss bound and establish strong queue stability when the net-service reserve exceeds the resulting loss coefficient. Numerical experiments show that both methods reduce normalized true-state pressure regret by about 70% relative to Point-estimate MP, while DR Regret MP yields lower network queues than direct robustification of absolute pressure. In microscopic SUMO experiments on a 28-intersection real-world network, Stochastic Regret MP and DR Regret MP reduce mean delay by 18.9% and 14.9%, respectively, and retain benefits under detector undercount and failure. The results show that representing uncertainty through its decision consequences can improve reliable traffic control under imperfect state information.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce a framework for analyzing and designing linear multirate control systems and their interconnections through the lens of robust control. The proposed approach models multirate systems as impulsive systems with multiple independent jump maps and corresponding jump-time sequences. We tackle analysis and design problems with stability, dissipativity, and robustness objectives, relying on a multi-clock-dependent Lyapunov function parameterization. Further, we develop a compositional approach for analyzing interconnections of multirate systems based on individual dissipativity properties. The theoretical conditions can be verified via linear matrix inequalities and they require weak assumptions on the sampling rates, allowing for asynchronous and time-varying sampling rates with or without separated time scales. The practicality of the framework is demonstrated with numerical examples.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can turn market notices and news into features for electricity price forecasting. Such features also record when documents arrive and whether their extraction failed, so a gain measured against a model without text can reflect document activity as well as content. We add the counts of the same documents and of their failed extractions to a no-text LightGBM model and measure each LLM representation against this activity reference, which needs no additional LLM requests. With gpt-6-luna as the extractor, we forecast Australian electricity prices from operator notices (5-30 minutes ahead) and WattClarity news (day-ahead) after the extractor's documented knowledge cutoff, averaging forecasts over three training seeds. For news, the counts alone lower mean absolute error (MAE) by 3.0%, most of the 3.6% reduction that the better LLM representation achieves relative to the no-text model; its increment beyond the counts is not significant. For notices, two of the three LLM representations lower MAE beyond the counts, by 0.14-0.21% and 0.45-0.52%, whether the counts are cumulative or cover recent windows, whereas the third shows no significant reduction. Time-shifted placebos give mixed evidence on whether these gains require text aligned with the target. Benchmarks using the operator's pre-dispatch forecasts (notices) or price history alone (news) are more accurate than all these text models. Gains against the no-text model and the activity reference differ in sign at individual seeds and, for notices with cumulative counts, in seed-averaged forecasts. Text gains should be reported against both references.

</details>

Abstract: This letter poses state estimation (SE) as an in-context tabular prediction (ICTP) problem. The key idea is a tabular arrangement in which historical measurement vectors and corresponding state estimates serve as the context, and a new measurement vector forms the query for which we predict a state vector. We propose to use tabular foundation models (TFMs), pretrained on synthetic data, whose weights are never updated, so power system data is needed only as context. With frozen TFM weights we estimate the state of 14-, 30-, 33-, 70-, and 118-bus transmission and distribution networks, and do so where weighted least squares (WLS) fails or degrades, under low observability and with bad data. ICTP errors are 11 to 57$\times$ below the per-bus average of the context at full metering, and 1.3 to 4.0$\times$ below it with only 5% of measurements available. With measurements at six buses biased by 8$σ$, the ICTP angle error rises 1.8 to 5.0$\times$ over its bias-free value against 2.5 to 12.7$\times$ for WLS with bad-data detection. Without retraining, ICTP has lower voltage error than DSS$^2$ on both of its feeders, and lower than a retrained TAPINE on two of three reconfiguration sets.

</details>

Abstract: We present a data-driven, three-phase framework for covert misappropriation attacks on closed-loop networked control systems. A man-in-the-middle (MITM) adversary, with access only to intercepted input-output data, incrementally identifies the plant's behavioral subspace while maintaining stealth under an infinity-norm intrusion-detection system (IDS). Phase 1 collects an initial rank-deficient Hankel matrix using a low-amplitude probing signal that satisfies an analytical stealth bound. Phase 2 adapts online rank-increasing experiment design to the closed-loop setting, using causal predictor-based masking to limit IDS residuals while the Hankel rank grows from 1 to the full behavioral rank n + mL in exactly n + mL - 1 steps. We show that the identified subspaces are strictly nested and that the projection error of any desired trajectory decreases monotonically, strictly so almost surely at each rank increase. Phase 3 employs the final Hankel matrix in a DeePC-style optimization to synthesize a covert attack that tracks an arbitrary reference while respecting the IDS constraint. Simulations on a DC motor with PI control demonstrate full-rank identification and stealthy tracking. Stealth is guaranteed analytically in Phases 1 and 3 and is numerically supported in Phase 2. The method is model-free and assumes noise-free LTI dynamics.

</details>
