Abstract: Accurate early-stage prediction of cascading failure severity is essential for assessing the vulnerability of power transmission networks. However, existing data-driven approaches often rely on network-specific representations, limiting their generalizability across power system topologies. This work investigates the complementary capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) for cascading failure severity prediction. First, standalone LLM predictors are developed using a LoRA-fine-tuned LLaMA-3-8B-Instruct model with varying degrees of topological information. A hybrid Graph-LLM fusion framework is then proposed, integrating a Topology-Aware Graph Attention Transformer (TAGAT) with the LLM through confidence-gated fusion. TAGAT captures structural dependencies among transmission lines, while the LLM extracts representations from early-stage power-flow observations and aggregated cascade descriptors. The frameworks are evaluated on 30,000 simulated cascading failure scenarios for each of the IEEE 118-bus and IEEE 300-bus systems. Using only the first four cascade steps, the hybrid framework achieves weighted F1 scores of 0.94 and 0.92, respectively. In cross-system transfer from IEEE 118-bus to IEEE 300-bus, it achieves 0.72 zero-shot accuracy, improving to 0.83 with limited target-system calibration, outperforming standalone models and conventional baselines. These results show the potential of combining graph-based structural learning with foundation models to improve cascading failure severity prediction and cross-system transferability.

</details>

Abstract: Advanced Driver Assistance Systems have become increasingly widespread, aiming to support drivers, reduce workload, and improve traffic safety. However, many systems are rarely used because their behavior feels unnatural and does not match individual driving behavior. The growing availability of large-scale, real-world human driving data enables more human-like and driver-individual assistance functions. This paper formulates the adaptation of Adaptive Cruise Control parameters as a driver-individual scenario-to-scenario transport problem, transforming controller parameters from one driving context to another. Using flow matching, we train a generative model to predict the target-scenario behavior of previously unseen drivers from only a few demonstrations in a source scenario. We compare the approach with a linear parameter-shift baseline and evaluate flow-matching variants incorporating driver-consistent coupling, driver embeddings, and classifier-free guidance based on driving profiles extracted from trajectory data using a variational autoencoder.

Abstract: Transmission expansion planning (TEP) seeks to identify cost-effective investment strategies that enable power systems to reliably meet future demand, while accounting for the location, timing, and operational impacts of infrastructure upgrades. As demand grows and renewable penetration increases, there is growing interest in jointly modeling energy storage investments to enhance operational flexibility. This work presents a multiperiod formulation that co-optimizes transmission reconductoring and energy storage installation across multiple planning periods. To solve the resulting large-scale problem efficiently, a Benders decomposition with per-period trust-region stabilization and a relaxation-based warm-start procedure is proposed. The approach is demonstrated on a 2,000-bus Texas system, producing an investment plan spanning 2030-2045 with optimality gap below 1.0%, and is compared against a rolling-horizon approach to highlight the benefits of multiperiod coordination.

</details>

Abstract: Machine learning for partial differential equations raises a practical question: when are classical, learned, or hybrid solvers justified? This survey compares the three families in a decision framework organized by data availability, query volume, and certification needs. An amortization law, tested on parametric Darcy flow, shows that surrogates trained on solver-generated data break even only after many queries, beyond thirty thousand for errors below one percent. An evidence ladder, a benchmark reanalysis, and three case studies show that claims of speedup and generalization carry the weakest evidence. Solver choice should follow regime, query volume, and evidence quality, not method novelty.

</details>

Abstract: Collective behavior in biological systems emerges through adaptive interactions among units, enabling large populations to coordinate without centralized control. This mechanism raises the question of whether collective behavior in engineered multi-agent systems can likewise be regulated by modifying the interaction law between agents, rather than by directly controlling individual units. To answer this question, we consider large-scale multi-agent systems evolving on a ring and treat the interaction kernel as a time-varying control variable whose evolution regulates the collective dynamics. Using a mean-field formulation, we develop a framework for the analysis and design of kernel-based controllers. We derive explicit conditions for controllability in application-relevant scenarios, and synthesize a state-feedback controller to regulate the kernel dynamics, validated on the microscopic, discrete dynamics. Numerical simulations demonstrate the approach's effectiveness and robustness to unmodeled perturbations. This work constitutes a first step in the design of bio-inspired control strategies for large-scale multi-agent systems by modulation of interactions between agents.

</details>

Abstract: Full-waveform inversion (FWI) reconstructs sound-speed fields from measured waveforms, by solving an optimization problem that minimizes the discrepancy between measured waveforms and those predicted by a numerical wave-propagation model. However, choosing suitable regularization for this optimization is challenging because it must stabilize the inversion while preserving relevant structures, prompting us to investigate whether priors learned from representative sound-speed images can improve reconstruction. In this work, we adapt plug-and-play flow matching (PnP-Flow) to FWI by alternating waveform-misfit updates with updates from a pretrained flow prior. We explore the effectiveness of PnP-Flow reconstruction by comparing it with total-variation (TV) regularization using noiseless transmission-only plane-wave data. Separate flow priors are trained on two circular-inclusion model families with constant and spatially varying backgrounds. We evaluate the method both on these in-distribution circle models and under substantial distribution shift by transferring the Gaussian-circle prior and reconstruction settings to breast phantom patches without retraining or retuning. Using the same number of waveform-misfit updates, the proposed PnP-Flow framework generally improves circle reconstruction accuracy and recovers breast lesion contrast more accurately than TV baselines.

</details>
